## Step 12. The number of arbitrary constants in the particular solution of a differential equation of third order are:

## Step 13. Formation of a Differential Equation whose General Solution is given

The equation 
$x^2+y^2+2x-4y+4=0$ 
[represents a circle having centre at $(-1, 2)$ and radius $1$ unit.]

--- Page 112 ---

## Step 1: Differentiate equation (1) with respect to $x$

Differentiating equation (1) with respect to $x$, we get

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x+1}{2-y} \quad (y \neq 2) \tag{2}
$$

## Step 2: Family of Concentric Circles

Let us consider the equation

$$
x^2 + y^2 = r^2 \tag{3}
$$

By giving different values to $r$, we get different members of the family e.g. $x^2+y^2=1, x^2+y^2=4, x^2+y^2=9$ etc. [A graph showing a series of concentric circles with radii 1, 2, and 3, centered at the origin].

Thus, equation (3) represents a family of concentric circles centered at the origin and having different radii.

## Step 3: Differential Equation for Family of Concentric Circles

We are interested in finding a differential equation that is satisfied by each member of the family. The differential equation must be free from $r$ because $r$ is different for different members of the family. This equation is obtained by differentiating equation (3) with respect to $x$, i.e.,

$$
2x + 2y \frac{dy}{dx} = 0 \quad \text{or} \quad x+y \frac{dy}{dx} = 0 \tag{4}
$$

which represents the family of concentric circles given by equation (3).

## Step 4: Family of Straight Lines

Again, let us consider the equation

$$
y = mx + c \tag{5}
$$

By giving different values to the parameters $m$ and $c$, we get different members of the family, e.g.,

$$
\begin{aligned}
y &= x & (m = 1, c = 0) \\
y &= \sqrt{3} x & (m = \sqrt{3}, c = 0) \\
y &= x + 1 & (m = 1, c = 1) \\
y &= -x & (m = -1, c = 0) \\
y &= -x - 1 & (m = -1, c = -1) 
\end{aligned}
$$

[A graph showing a series of straight lines with different slopes and y-intercepts].

Thus, equation (5) represents the family of straight lines, where $m, c$ are parameters.

## Step 5: Differential Equation for Family of Straight Lines

We are now interested in finding a differential equation that is satisfied by each member of the family. Further, the equation must be free from $m$ and $c$ because $m$ and $c$ are different for different members of the family.

--- Page 113 ---

### Transcribed Textbook Page in Clean Markdown

$c$ are different for different members of the family. This is obtained by differentiating equation $(5)$ with respect to $x$, successively we get

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = m \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 0 \quad ... (6)
$$

The equation $(6)$ represents the family of straight lines given by equation $(5)$.

Note that equations $(3)$ and $(5)$ are the general solutions of equations $(4)$ and $(6)$ respectively.

### 9.4.1 Procedure to form a differential equation that will represent a given family of curves

**(a) If the given family $\mathrm{F}_{1}$ of curves depends on only one parameter then it is represented by an equation of the form**

$$
\mathrm{F}_{1}(x,y,a) = 0 \quad ... (1)
$$

For example, the family of parabolas $y^2 = ax$ can be represented by an equation of the form $f(x, y, a) : y^2 = ax$.

Differentiating equation $(1)$ with respect to $x$, we get an equation involving $y',y,x,$ and $a$, i.e.,

$$
g \left( x, y, y', a \right) = 0 \quad ... (2)
$$

The required differential equation is then obtained by eliminating $a$ from equations $(1)$ and $(2)$ as

$$
\mathrm{F} \left( x, y, y' \right) = 0 \quad ... (3)
$$

**(b) If the given family $\mathrm{F}_{2}$ of curves depends on the parameters $a, b$ (say) then it is represented by an equation of the form**

$$
\mathrm{F}_{2}(x,y,a,b) = 0 \quad ... (4)
$$

Differentiating equation $(4)$ with respect to $x$, we get an equation involving $y',x,y,a,$ and $b$, i.e.,

$$
g \left( x, y, y', a, b \right) = 0 \quad ... (5)
$$

But it is not possible to eliminate two parameters $a$ and $b$ from the two equations and so, we need a third equation. This equation is obtained by differentiating equation $(5)$, with respect to $x$, to obtain a relation of the form

$$
h \left( x, y, y', y'', a, b \right) = 0 \quad ... (6)
$$

[The image contains a graph/figure showing a family of straight lines with different slopes, intersecting at the origin, with labels $y = \sqrt{3}x$, $y = x$, $y = x+1$, $y = -x$, and $y = - \sqrt{3}x$.]

--- Page 114 ---

## Mathematics

The required differential equation is then obtained by eliminating $a$ and $b$ from equations (4), (5) and (6) as
$F \left(x,y,y',y''\right) = 0$ 

[No diagram present on this page]

### Example 4
Form the differential equation representing the family of curves $y = mx$, where, $m$ is arbitrary constant.

#### Solution
We have
$y = mx$ 
or 
$y' = m$

Substituting the value of $m$ in equation (1) we get $y = y' \cdot x$ 
or 
$x y' - y = 0$

which is free from the parameter $m$ and hence this is the required differential equation.

### Example 5
Form the differential equation representing the family of curves $y = a \sin (x + b)$, where $a$, $b$ are arbitrary constants.

#### Solution
We have 
$y = a \sin (x + b)$ 

Differentiating both sides of equation (1) with respect to $x$, successively we get
$y' = a \cos (x + b)$ 
or 
$y'' = -a \sin (x + b)$

Eliminating $a$ and $b$ from equations (1), (2) and (3), we get 
$y'' + y = 0$

which is free from the arbitrary constants $a$ and $b$ and hence this the required differential equation.

--- Page 115 ---

## Example 6
Form the differential equation representing the family of ellipses having foci on $x$-axis and centre at the origin.

### Solution
We know that the equation of said family of ellipses (see [a family of ellipses with major axis along the x-axis and center at the origin]) is
$$
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \tag{1}
$$

Differentiating equation (1) with respect to $x$, we get
$$
\frac{2x}{a^2} + \frac{2y}{b^2} \frac{dy}{dx} = 0
$$
or
$$
\frac{y}{x} \left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right) = -\frac{b^2}{a^2} \tag{2}
$$

Differentiating both sides of equation (2) with respect to $x$, we get
$$
\left(\frac{y}{x}\right) \left(\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}\right) + \left(\frac{x \frac{dy}{dx} - y}{x^2}\right) \frac{dy}{dx} = 0
$$
or
$$
xy \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + x\left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)^2 - y \frac{dy}{dx} = 0 \tag{3}
$$
which is the required differential equation.

## Example 7
Form the differential equation of the family of circles touching the $x$-axis at origin.

### Solution
Let $C$ denote the family of circles touching $x$-axis at origin. Let $(0, a)$ be the coordinates of the centre of any member of the family (see [a family of circles touching the x-axis at the origin]). Therefore, equation of family $C$ is
$$
x^2 + (y - a)^2 = a^2 \quad \text{or} \quad x^2 + y^2 = 2ay \tag{1}
$$
where, $a$ is an arbitrary constant. Differentiating both sides of equation (1) with respect to $x$, we get
$$
2x + 2y \frac{dy}{dx} = 2a \frac{dy}{dx}
$$

## 9.5. Methods of Solving First Order, First Degree Differential Equations

In this section we shall discuss three methods of solving first order first degree differential equations.

### 9.5.1 Differential equations with variables separable

A first order-first degree differential equation is of the form

$\frac{dy}{dx} = \text{F}(x, y)$  $\ldots (1)$

--- Page 118 ---

## Variable Separable Type

If $\mathrm{F}(x, y)$ can be expressed as a product $\mathrm{g}(x) h(y)$, where, $g(x)$ is a function of $x$ and $h(y)$ is a function of $y$, then the differential equation 
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = h(y) \cdot g(x)$$
is said to be of variable separable type. The differential equation then has the form

If $h(y) \neq 0$, separating the variables, it can be rewritten as 
$$\frac{1}{h(y)} dy = g(x) dx$$

Integrating both sides, we get 
$$\int \frac{1}{h(y)} dy = \int g(x) dx$$

Thus, it provides the solutions of given differential equation in the form 
$$\mathrm{H}(y) = \mathrm{G}(x) + \mathrm{C}$$
Here, $\mathrm{H} (y)$ and $\mathrm{G} (x)$ are the anti derivatives of $\frac{1}{h(y)}$ and $g(x)$ respectively and $\mathrm{C}$ is the arbitrary constant.

### Example 9

Find the general solution of the differential equation 
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x+1}{2-y}, (y\neq2)$$

#### Solution

We have 
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x+1}{2-y}$$
Separating the variables in equation, we get 
$$(2-y) dy = (x + 1) dx$$
Integrating both sides, we get 
$$\int(2-y) dy = \int(x+1) dx$$
or 
$$2y - \frac{y^2}{2} = \frac{x^2}{2} + x + \mathrm{C}_1$$
or 
$$x^2 + y^2 + 2x - 4y + 2 \mathrm{C}_1 = 0$$
or 
$$x^2 + y^2 + 2x - 4y + \mathrm{C} = 0$$
which is the general solution of equation.

--- Page 119 ---

### Differential Equations

#### Example 10
Find the general solution of the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1+y^2}{1+x^2}$.

Since $1 + y^2 \neq 0$, therefore separating the variables, the given differential equation can be written as
$$
\frac{dy}{1+y^2} = \frac{dx}{1+x^2} \tag{1}
$$
[No diagram]

Integrating both sides of equation (1), we get
$$
\int \frac{dy}{1+y^2} = \int \frac{dx}{1+x^2}
$$
or
$$
\tan^{-1} y = \tan^{-1} x + C
$$
which is the general solution of equation (1).

#### Example 11
Find the particular solution of the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = -4xy^2$ given that $y = 1$, when $x = 0$.

Solution If $y \neq 0$, the given differential equation can be written as
$$
\frac{dy}{y^2} = -4x dx \tag{1}
$$
[No diagram]

Integrating both sides of equation (1), we get
$$
\int \frac{dy}{y^2} = -4 \int x dx
$$
or
$$
-\frac{1}{y} = -2x^2 + C
$$
or
$$
y = \frac{1}{2x^2 - C} \tag{2}
$$
Substituting $y = 1$ and $x = 0$ in equation (2), we get, $C = -1$.

Now substituting the value of C in equation (2), we get the particular solution of the given differential equation as $y = \frac{1}{2x^2 + 1}$.

#### Example 12
Find the equation of the curve passing through the point $(1, 1)$ whose differential equation is $x dy = (2x^2 + 1) dx$ ($x \neq 0$).

--- Page 120 ---

## Step 1: Convert the first equation to LaTeX
The first equation is $dy^* = \left(\frac{2x^2 + 1}{x}\right) dx^*$, which can be written as $dy^* = \left(\frac{2x^2 + 1}{x}\right) dx^*$.

## Step 2: Convert the second equation to LaTeX
The second equation is $dy = \left(2x + \frac{1}{x}\right) dx$, which can be written as $dy = \left(2x + \frac{1}{x}\right) dx$.

## Step 3: Convert the integral equation to LaTeX
The integral equation is $\int dy = \int\left(2x+\frac{1}{x}\right) dx$, which can be written as $\int dy = \int\left(2x+\frac{1}{x}\right) dx$.

## 4: Convert the result of the integral equation to LaTeX
The result of the integral equation is $y = x^2 + \log |x| + C$, which can be written as $y = x^2 + \log |x| + C$.

## 5: Convert the equation of the curve to LaTeX
The equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2x}{y^2}$ can be written as $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2x}{y^2}$.

## 6: Convert the separated variables equation to LaTeX
The separated variables equation is $y^2 dy = 2x dx$, which can be written as $y^2 dy = 2x dx$.

## 7: Convert the integral of the separated variables equation to LaTeX
The integral of the separated variables equation is $\int y^2 dy = \int 2x dx$, which can be written as $\int y^2 dy = \int 2x dx$.

## 8: Convert the result of the integral of the separated variables equation to LaTeX
The result of the integral of the separated variables equation is $\frac{y^3}{3} = x^2 + C$, which can be written as $\frac{y^3}{3} = x^2 + C$.

The given differential equation can be expressed as 
$dy^* = \left(\frac{2x^2 + 1}{x}\right) dx^*$

or 
$dy = \left(2x + \frac{1}{x}\right) dx$ ... (1)

Integrating both sides of equation (1), we get
$\int dy = \int\left(2x+\frac{1}{x}\right) dx$

$y = x^2 + \log |x| + C$ ... (2)

Equation (2) represents the family of solution curves of the given differential equation but we are interested in finding the equation of a particular member of the family which passes through the point $(1, 1)$. Therefore substituting $x = 1, y = 1$ in equation (2), we get $C = 0$.

Now substituting the value of $C$ in equation (2) we get the equation of the required curve as $y = x^2 + \log |x|$.

## Example 13 
Find the equation of a curve passing through the point $(-2, 3)$, given that the slope of the tangent to the curve at any point $(x,y)$ is $\frac{2x}{y^2}$.

## Solution 
We know that the slope of the tangent to a curve is given by $\frac{dy}{dx}$. 
so, 
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2x}{y^2}$ ... (1)

Separating the variables, equation (1) can be written as 
$y^2 dy = 2x dx$ ... (2)

Integrating both sides of equation (2), we get
$\int y^2 dy = \int 2x dx$

$\frac{y^3}{3} = x^2 + C$ ... (3)

## Step 1: Substitute $x=-2, y=3$ in equation (3) to find C

Substituting $x=-2, y=3$ in equation (3), we get $C=5$.

## Step 2: Find the equation of the required curve

Substituting the value of C in equation (3), we get the equation of the required curve as 
$\frac{y^3}{3} = x^2+5$ or $y=(3x^2+15)^{\frac{1}{3}}$.

## Step 3: Describe the problem of Example 14

[No diagram or graph is present on this page, but there is a description of a problem involving a bank with a principal amount that increases continuously at a rate of 5% per year.]

## Step 4: Solve Example 14

### Step 4.1: Define the problem and the differential equation

Let P be the principal at any time $t$. According to the given problem,
$\frac{dP}{dt} = (\frac{5}{100}) \times P$

or 
$\frac{dP}{dt} = \frac{P}{20}$  ... (1)

### Step 4.2: Separate the variables

separating the variables in equation (1), we get 
$\frac{dP}{P} = \frac{dt}{20}$  ... (2)

### Step 4.3: Integrate both sides of equation (2)

Integrating both sides of equation (2), we get 
$\log P = \frac{t}{20}+C_1$

or 
$P = e^{\frac{t}{20}} \cdot e^{C_1}$

or 
$P = C e^{\frac{t}{20}}$ (where $e^{C_1}=C$)  ... (3)

### Step 4.4: Find the value of C

Now 
$P=1000$, when $t=0$

Substituting the values of P and $t$ in (3), we get $C=1000$. Therefore, equation (3), gives 
$P = 1000 e^{\frac{t}{20}}$

### Step 4.5: Find the time required to double the principal

Let $t$ years be the time required to double the principal. Then 
$2000 = 1000 e^{\frac{t}{20}} \Rightarrow t=20 \log_e 2$

## Step 9.5.2 Homogeneous differential equations

Consider the following functions in $x$ and $y$

$\mathrm{F}_{1}(x,y) = y^2 + 2xy$, $\quad \mathrm{F}_{2}(x,y) = 2x - 3y,$ 

$\mathrm{F}_{3}(x,y) = \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right), \quad \mathrm{F}_{4}(x,y) = \sin x + \cos y$

If we replace $x$ and $y$ by $\lambda x$ and $\lambda y$ respectively in the above functions, for any nonzero constant $\lambda$, we get

$\mathrm{F}_{1}(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \lambda^2 (y^2 + 2xy) = \lambda^2 \mathrm{F}_{1}(x, y)$

$\mathrm{F}_{2}(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \lambda (2x - 3y) = \lambda \mathrm{F}_{2}(x, y)$

$\mathrm{F}_{3}(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \cos \left(\frac{\lambda y}{\lambda x}\right) = \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) = \lambda^0 \mathrm{F}_{3}(x, y)$

$\mathrm{F}_{4}(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \sin \lambda x + \cos \lambda y \neq \lambda^n \mathrm{F}_{4}(x, y)$, for any $n \in \mathbb{N}$

Here, we observe that the functions $\mathrm{F}_{1}$, $\mathrm{F}_{2}$, $\mathrm{F}_{3}$ can be written in the form $\mathrm{F}(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \lambda^n \mathrm{F}(x, y)$ but $\mathrm{F}_{4}$ cannot be written in this form. This leads to the following definition:

A function $\mathrm{F}(x, y)$ is said to be *homogeneous function* of degree $n$ if 

$\mathrm{F}(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \lambda^n \mathrm{F}(x, y)$ for any nonzero constant $\lambda$.

We note that in the above examples, $\mathrm{F}_{1}$, $\mathrm{F}_{2}$, $\mathrm{F}_{3}$ are homogeneous functions of degree $2$, $1$, $0$ respectively but $\mathrm{F}_{4}$ is not a homogeneous function.

## Exercises

20. In a bank, principal increases continuously at the rate of $r\%$ per year. Find the value of $r$ if Rs $100$ double itself in $10$ years ($\log_{e}2 = 0.6931$).

Let $P(t)$ be the principal at time $t$. Then,

$\frac{dP}{dt} = \frac{r}{100}P$

$\Rightarrow \frac{dP}{P} = \frac{r}{100}dt$

$\int \frac{dP}{P} = \int \frac{r}{100}dt$

$\log P = \frac{r}{100}t + C$

$P = e^{\frac{r}{100}t + C} = e^C e^{\frac{r}{100}t}$

$P = A e^{\frac{r}{100}t}$, where $A = e^C$

Given, $P(0) = 100$, $P(10) = 200$

$100 = A e^0 \Rightarrow A = 100$

$200 = 100 e^{\frac{r}{10}}$

$\Rightarrow e^{\frac{r}{10}} = 2$

$\Rightarrow \frac{r}{10} = \log_e 2$

$\Rightarrow r = 10 \log_e 2 = 10 \times 0.6931 = 6.931$

## [No diagram]

21. In a bank, principal increases continuously at the rate of $5\%$ per year. An amount of Rs $1000$ is deposited with this bank, how much will it worth after $10$ years ($e^{0.5} = 1.648$).

Let $P(t)$ be the principal at time $t$. Then,

$\frac{dP}{dt} = \frac{5}{100}P$

$\Rightarrow \frac{dP}{P} = \frac{1}{20}dt$

$\int \frac{dP}{P} = \int \frac{1}{20}dt$

$\log P = \frac{1}{20}t + C$

$P = e^{\frac{1}{20}t + C} = e^C e^{\frac{1}{20}t}$

$P = A e^{\frac{1}{20}t}$, where $A = e^C$

Given, $P(0) = 1000$

$1000 = A e^0 \Rightarrow A = 1000$

$P(10) = 1000 e^{\frac{1}{2}} = 1000 \times 1.648 = 1648$

## [No diagram]

22. In a culture, the bacteria count is $1,00,000$. The number is increased by $10\%$ in $2$ hours. In how many hours will the count reach $2,00,000$, if the rate of growth of bacteria is proportional to the number present?

Let $N(t)$ be the number of bacteria at time $t$. Then,

$\frac{dN}{dt} = kN$

$\Rightarrow \frac{dN}{N} = kdt$

$\int \frac{dN}{N} = \int kdt$

$\log N = kt + C$

$N = e^{kt + C} = e^C e^{kt}$

$N = A e^{kt}$, where $A = e^C$

Given, $N(0) = 100000$, $N(2) = 110000$

$100000 = A e^0 \Rightarrow A = 100000$

$110000 = 100000 e^{2k}$

$\Rightarrow e^{2k} = 1.1$

$\Rightarrow 2k = \log_e 1.1$

$\Rightarrow k = \frac{1}{2} \log_e 1.1$

$N(t) = 200000$

$200000 = 100000 e^{\frac{1}{2} \log_e 1.1 t}$

$\Rightarrow e^{\frac{1}{2} \log_e 1.1 t} = 2$

$\Rightarrow \frac{1}{2} \log_e 1.1 t = \log_e 2$

$\Rightarrow \log_e 1.1 t = 2 \log_e 2$

$\Rightarrow \log_e 1.1 t = \log_e 4$

$\Rightarrow 1.1 t = 4$

$\Rightarrow t = \frac{4}{1.1}$

$\Rightarrow -e^{-y} = e^x + C$

## Step 3: Rearrange the equation
$e^{-y} + e^x = -C$

## Step 4: Write the general solution

## Differential Equations

### General Solution of Homogeneous Differential Equations

The general solution of a homogeneous differential equation can be found using the following steps:

$$
v + x \frac{dv}{dx} = g(v)
$$

or 

$$
x \frac{dv}{dx} = g(v) - v
$$
... (4)

Separating the variables in equation (4), we get

$$
\frac{dv}{g(v)-v} = \frac{dx}{x}
$$
... (5)

Integrating both sides of equation (5), we get

$$
\int \frac{dv}{g(v)-v} = \int \frac{1}{x} dx + C
$$
... (6)

Equation (6) gives the general solution (primitive) of the differential equation (1) when we replace $v$ by $\frac{y}{x}$.

[No diagrams or graphs are present on this page, but if there were, they would be described here.]

### Note

If the homogeneous differential equation is in the form $\frac{dx}{dy} = F(x,y)$ where $F(x,y)$ is a homogeneous function of degree zero, then we make the substitution $\frac{x}{y} = v$ i.e., $x=vy$ and we proceed further to find the general solution as discussed above by writing $\frac{dx}{dy} = h(\frac{x}{y})$.

## Step 4: Example 16

### Example 16

Show that the differential equation $x \cos(\frac{y}{x})\frac{dy}{dx} = y \cos(\frac{y}{x}) + x$ is homogeneous and solve it.

### Solution

The given differential equation can be written as 

$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y \cos(\frac{y}{x}) + x}{x \cos(\frac{y}{x})}$

--- Page 128 ---

## Step 1: Identify the differential equation
The differential equation given is of the form $\frac{dy}{dx} = F(x, y)$, where $F(x,y) = \frac{y \cos(\frac{y}{x}) + x}{x \cos(\frac{y}{x})}$.

## Step 2: Determine if $F(x, y)$ is homogeneous
To check if $F(x, y)$ is homogeneous, we replace $x$ by $\lambda x$ and $y$ by $\lambda y$:
$F(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \frac{\lambda y \cos(\frac{\lambda y}{\lambda x}) + \lambda x}{\lambda x \cos(\frac{\lambda y}{\lambda x})} = \frac{\lambda [y \cos(\frac{y}{x}) + x]}{\lambda (x \cos(\frac{y}{x}))} = \lambda^0 [F(x, y)]$.
Thus, $F(x, y)$ is a homogeneous function of degree zero.

## 3: Solve the differential equation
Given that the differential equation is homogeneous, we make the substitution $y = vx$.

## 4: Differentiate $y = vx$ with respect to $x$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = v + x \frac{dv}{dx}$.

## 5: Substitute $y$ and $\frac{dy}{dx}$ into the original differential equation
Substituting into $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y \cos(\frac{y}{x}) + x}{x \cos(\frac{y}{x})}$ gives:
$v + x \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{vx \cos(v) + x}{x \cos(v)}$.

## 6: Simplify the equation
$v + x \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v \cos(v) + 1}{\cos(v)}$.

## 7: Solve for $\frac{dv}{dx}$
$x \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v \cos(v) + 1}{\cos(v)} - v$.

## 8: Further simplification
$x \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v \cos(v) + 1 - v \cos(v)}{\cos(v)} = \frac{1}{\cos(v)}$.

## 9: Separate variables
$\cos(v) dv = \frac{dx}{x}$.

## 10: Integrate both sides
$\int \cos(v) dv = \int \frac{1}{x} dx$.

The final answer is: 

It is a differential equation of the form $\frac{dy}{dx}=F(x,y)$. 
Here 
$$F(x,y) = \frac{y \cos(\frac{y}{x}) + x}{x \cos(\frac{y}{x})}.$$
Replacing $x$ by $\lambda x$ and $y$ by $\lambda y$, we get
$$F(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \frac{\lambda[y \cos(\frac{y}{x})+x]}{\lambda(x \cos(\frac{y}{x}))} = \lambda^0 [F(x,y)].$$
Thus, $F(x, y)$ is a homogeneous function of degree zero. 
Therefore, the given differential equation is a homogeneous differential equation. 
To solve it we make the substitution 
$$y=vx \tag{2}$$
Differentiating equation (2) with respect to $x$, we get 
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = v + x \frac{dv}{dx} \tag{3}$$
Substituting the value of $y$ and $\frac{dy}{dx}$ in equation (1), we get 
$$v + x \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v \cos v + 1}{\cos v}$$
or 
$$x \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v \cos v + 1}{\cos v} - v$$
or 
$$x \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{1}{\cos v}$$
or 
$$\cos v dv = \frac{dx}{x}$$
Therefore 
$$\int \cos v dv = \int \frac{1}{x} dx.$$

### Example 17

Show that the differential equation $2y e^{\frac{x}{y}} dx + (y-2x e^{\frac{x}{y}}) dy=0$ is homogeneous and find its particular solution, given that, $x = 0$ when $y = 1$.

#### Solution

$\frac{dx}{dy} = \frac{2xe^{\frac{x}{y}}-y}{2ye^{\frac{x}{y}}}$  ...(1)

Let

$F(x,y) = \frac{2xe^{\frac{x}{y}}-y}{2ye^{\frac{x}{y}}}$

Then

$F(\lambda x, \lambda y) = \frac{\lambda (2xe^{\frac{x}{y}}-y)}{\lambda (2ye^{\frac{x}{y}})} = \lambda^0 [F(x,y)]$

Thus, $F(x, y)$ is a homogeneous function of degree zero. Therefore, the given differential equation is a homogeneous differential equation.

$x = vy$  ...(2)

$\frac{dx}{dy} = v + y \frac{dv}{dy}$

## Step 1: Substitute the value of $x$ and $\frac{dx}{dy}$ in equation (1)

Substituting the value of $x$ and $\frac{dx}{dy}$ in equation (1), we get

$$v + y \frac{dv}{dy} = \frac{2ve^v - 1}{2e^v}$$

or

$$y \frac{dv}{dy} = \frac{2ve^v - 1}{2e^v} - v$$

or

$$y \frac{dv}{dy} = -\frac{1}{2e^v}$$

or

$$2e^v dv = -\frac{dy}{y}$$

or

$$\int 2e^v \cdot dv = -\int \frac{dy}{y}$$

or

$$2 e^v = -\log|y| + C$$

and replacing $v$ by $\frac{x}{y}$, we get

$$2 e^{\frac{x}{y}} + \log|y| = C \tag{3}$$

## Step 2: Find the particular solution of the given differential equation

Substituting $x = 0$ and $y = 1$ in equation (3), we get

$$2 e^0 + \log|1| = C \Rightarrow C = 2$$

Substituting the value of $C$ in equation (3), we get

$$2 e^{\frac{x}{y}} + \log|y| = 2$$

which is the particular solution of the given differential equation.

$$\int \frac{2v}{v^2-1} dv = -\int \frac{1}{x} dx$$

$$\log |v^2-1| = -\log |x| + \log |C_1|$$

$$\log |(v^2-1)(x)| = \log |C_1|$$

$$(v^2-1)x = \pm C_1$$

## Step 2: Describe graphs/figures in brackets

There are no graphs or figures in the given text.

## Step 3: Clean the text

The cleaned text is:

The given differential equation is:

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1+\frac{y^2}{x^2}}{2y} \over x$$

or

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1 + \frac{y^2}{x^2}}{2y/x} = \frac{x^2 + y^2}{2xy}.$$

Clearly, it is a homogenous differential equation. To solve it, we make the substitution

$$y = vx$$

Differentiating $y = vx$ with respect to $x$, we get

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = v + x\frac{dv}{dx}$$

or

$$v + x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{1+v^2}{2v}$$

or

$$x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{1-v^2}{2v}$$

or

$$\frac{2v}{1-v^2}dv = \frac{dx}{x}$$

or

$$\frac{2v}{v^2-1}dv = -\frac{dx}{x}$$

Therefore

$$\int \frac{2v}{v^2-1} dv = -\int \frac{1}{x} dx$$

or

$$\log |v^2-1| = -\log |x| + \log |C_1|$$

or

$$\log |(v^2-1)(x)| = \log |C_1|$$

or

$$(v^2-1)x = \pm C_1$$

Replacing $v$ by $\frac{y}{x}$, we get

$$\left(\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^2 - 1\right)x = \pm C_1$$

or

$$\left(\frac{y^2}{x^2} - 1\right)x = \pm C_1$$

or

$$\left(y^2 - x^2\right) = \pm C_1 x$$

or

$$x^2 - y^2 = C x$$

## Exercise 9.5

### Section 1: Homogeneous Differential Equations

Show that the given differential equation is homogeneous and solve each of them.

1. $(x^2+xy) dy=(x^2+y^2) dx$

2. $y'=\frac{x+y}{x}$

3. $(x-y) dy-(x+y) dx=0$

4. $(x^2-y^2) dx+2xy dy=0$

5. $x^2 \frac{dy}{dx}=x^2-2y^2+xy$

6. $x dy-y dx=\sqrt{x^2+y^2} dx$

7. $\left\{x\cos\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)+y\sin\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\right\} y dx=\left\{y\sin\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)-x\cos\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\right\} x dy$

8. $x \frac{dy}{dx}-y+x\sin\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)=0$

9. $y dx+x \log\left(\frac{y}{x}\right) dy-2x dy=0$

10. $\left(1+e^{\frac{y}{x}}\right) dx+e^{\frac{y}{x}}\left(1-\frac{x}{y}\right) dy=0$

### Section 2: Particular Solutions

For each of the differential equations in Exercises from 11 to 15, find the particular solution satisfying the given condition:

11. $(x+y) dy+(x-y) dx=0; y=1$ when $x=1$

12. $x^2 dy+(xy+y^2) dx=0; y=1$ when $x=1$

13. $\left[x\sin^2\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)-y\right] dx+x dy=0; y=\frac{\pi}{4}$ when $x=1$

[No diagram present]

14. $\frac{dy}{dx}-\frac{y}{x}+\csc\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)=0; y=0$ when $x=1$

15. $2xy+y^2-2x^2 \frac{dy}{dx}=0; y=2$ when $x=1$

### Section 3: Substitution Method

16. A homogeneous differential equation of the form $\frac{dx}{dy}=h\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)$ can be solved by making the substitution.

[No diagram present]

## Solution

## Section 1: Homogeneous Differential Equations

1. $(x^2+xy) dy=(x^2+y^2) dx$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x^2+y^2}{x^2+xy}$

   Let $x=vy \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{v+x\frac{dv}{dx}}$

   $\implies \frac{1}{v+x\frac{dv}{dx}} = \frac{v^2+1}{v^2+v}$

   $\implies \int \frac{v^2+v}{v^2+1} dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \int (1 + \frac{v}{v^2+1}) dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies v + \frac{1}{2} \ln(v^2+1) = \ln x + C$

   $\implies \frac{y}{x} + \frac{1}{2} \ln(\frac{y^2}{x^2}+1) = \ln x + C$

2. $y'=\frac{x+y}{x}$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = 1+\frac{y}{x}$

   Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

   $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = 1+v$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = 1$

   $\implies \int dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies v = \ln x + C$

   $\implies \frac{y}{x} = \ln x + C$

   $\implies y = x\ln x + Cx$

3. $(x-y) dy-(x+y) dx=0$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x+y}{x-y}$

   Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

   $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{1+v}{1-v}$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{1+v}{1-v} - v$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{1+v-v+v^2}{1-v}$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{1+v^2}{1-v}$

   $\implies \int \frac{1-v}{1+v^2} dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \int \frac{1}{1+v^2} dv - \int \frac{v}{1+v^2} dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \tan^{-1}v - \frac{1}{2} \ln(1+v^2) = \ln x + C$

   $\implies \tan^{-1}(\frac{y}{x}) - \frac{1}{2} \ln(1+\frac{y^2}{x^2}) = \ln x + C$

4. $(x^2-y^2) dx+2xy dy=0$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y^2-x^2}{2xy}$

   Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

   $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v^2-1}{2v}$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v^2-1}{2v} - v$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v^2-1-2v^2}{2v}$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{-(1+v^2)}{2v}$

   $\implies \int \frac{2v}{1+v^2} dv = -\int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \ln(1+v^2) = -\ln x + C$

   $\implies \ln(1+\frac{y^2}{x^2}) = -\ln x + C$

   $\implies \ln(x^2+y^2) = C$

5. $x^2 \frac{dy}{dx}=x^2-2y^2+xy$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = 1-2(\frac{y}{x})^2+\frac{y}{x}$

   Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

   $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = 1-2v^2+v$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = 1-2v^2$

   $\implies \int \frac{dv}{1-2v^2} = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \tanh^{-1}(\sqrt{2}v) = \ln x + C$

   $\implies \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \tanh^{-1}(\sqrt{2}\frac{y}{x}) = \ln x + C$

6. $x dy-y dx=\sqrt{x^2+y^2} dx$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y}{x} + \sqrt{1+(\frac{y}{x})^2}$

   Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

   $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = v + \sqrt{1+v^2}$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \sqrt{1+v^2}$

   $\implies \int \frac{dv}{\sqrt{1+v^2}} = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \ln(v+\sqrt{1+v^2}) = \ln x + C$

   $\implies \ln(\frac{y}{x}+\sqrt{1+\frac{y^2}{x^2}}) = \ln x + C$

7. $\left\{x\cos\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)+y\sin\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\right\} y dx=\left\{y\sin\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)-x\cos\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\right\} x dy$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x}{y} \frac{x\cos(\frac{y}{x})+y\sin(\frac{y}{x})}{y\sin(\frac{y}{x})-x\cos(\frac{y}{x})}$

   Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

   $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{\cos(v)+v\sin(v)}{v\sin(v)-\cos(v)}v$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v\cos(v)+v^2\sin(v)-v^2\sin(v)+v\cos(v)}{v\sin(v)-\cos(v)}$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{2v\cos(v)}{v\sin(v)-\cos(v)}$

   $\implies \int \frac{v\sin(v)-\cos(v)}{v\cos(v)} dv = 2\int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \int (\frac{\sin(v)}{v} - \frac{\cos(v)}{v^2} \frac{v}{\cos(v)}) dv = 2\int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \int (\frac{\sin(v)}{v} - \frac{\cos(v)}{v}) dv = 2\int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \int \frac{\sin(v)}{v} dv - \int \frac{\cos(v)}{v} dv = 2\int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies Si(v) - Ci(v) = 2\ln x + C$

   $\implies Si(\frac{y}{x}) - Ci(\frac{y}{x}) = 2\ln x + C$

8. $x \frac{dy}{dx}-y+x\sin\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)=0$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y}{x} - \sin(\frac{y}{x})$

   Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

   $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = v - \sin(v)$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = -\sin(v)$

   $\implies \int \csc(v) dv = -\int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \ln|\csc(v) - \cot(v)| = -\ln x + C$

   $\implies \ln|\csc(\frac{y}{x}) - \cot(\frac{y}{x})| = -\ln x + C$

9. $y dx+x \log\left(\frac{y}{x}\right) dy-2x dy=0$

   $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y}{x(2-\log(\frac{y}{x}))}$

   Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

   $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v}{2-\log(v)}$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v}{2-\log(v)} - v$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v-2v+v\log(v)}{2-\log(v)}$

   $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v\log(v)-v}{2-\log(v)}$

   $\implies \int \frac{2-\log(v)}{v(\log(v)-1)} dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies \int (\frac{2}{v(\log(v)-1)} - \frac{1}{v}) dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies 2\int \frac{1}{v(\log(v)-1)} dv - \int \frac{1}{v} dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

   $\implies 2\ln|\log(v)-1| - \ln|v| = \ln x + C$

   $\implies \ln|(\log(\frac{y}{x})-1)^2| - \ln|\frac{y}{x}| = \ln x + C$

10. $\left(1+e^{\frac{y}{x}}\right) dx+e^{\frac{y}{x}}\left(1-\frac{x}{y}\right) dy=0$

    $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y(1+e^{\frac{y}{x}})}{x(e^{\frac{y}{x}}-1)}$

    Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

    $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v(1+e^v)}{e^v-1}$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v(1+e^v)}{e^v-1} - v$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{ve^v-v}{e^v-1}$

    $\implies \int \frac{e^v-1}{v} dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

    $\implies \int \frac{e^v}{v} dv - \int \frac{1}{v} dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

    $\implies Ei(v) - \ln|v| = \ln x + C$

    $\implies Ei(\frac{y}{x}) - \ln|\frac{y}{x}| = \ln x + C$

## Section 2: Particular Solutions

11. $(x+y) dy+(x-y) dx=0; y=1$ when $x=1$

    $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y-x}{x+y}$

    Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

    $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v-1}{v+1}$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v-1}{v+1} - v$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v-1-v^2-v}{v+1}$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{-v^2-1}{v+1}$

    $\implies \int \frac{v+1}{v^2+1} dv = -\int \frac{dx}{x}$

    $\implies \int \frac{v}{v^2+1} dv + \int \frac{1}{v^2+1} dv = -\int \frac{dx}{x}$

    $\implies \frac{1}{2} \ln(v^2+1) + \tan^{-1}v = -\ln x + C$

    $\implies \frac{1}{2} \ln((\frac{y}{x})^2+1) + \tan^{-1}(\frac{y}{x}) = -\ln x + C$

    Given $y(1)=1 \implies \frac{1}{2} \ln(2) + \frac{\pi}{4} = -0 + C$

    $\implies C = \frac{\pi}{4} + \frac{1}{2} \ln(2)$

    $\implies \frac{1}{2} \ln((\frac{y}{x})^2+1) + \tan^{-1}(\frac{y}{x}) = -\ln x + \frac{\pi}{4} + \frac{1}{2} \ln(2)$

12. $x^2 dy+(xy+y^2) dx=0; y=1$ when $x=1$

    $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{y(x+y)}{x^2}$

    Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

    $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = -v(\frac{1+v}{x})$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = -v(\frac{1+v}{x}) - v$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = -v(\frac{1+v}{x} + 1)$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = -v(\frac{1+v+x}{x})$

    $\implies \int \frac{dv}{v} + \int \frac{1+v+x}{x} dx = 0$

    $\implies \ln|v| + \int ( \frac{1}{x} + \frac{v}{x} + 1) dx = 0$

    $\implies \ln|\frac{y}{x}| + \ln|x| + \int ( \frac{y}{x^2} + 1) dx = 0$

    $\implies \ln|y| + \int ( \frac{y}{x^2} + 1) dx = 0$

    Given $y(1)=1 \implies \ln|1| + \int ( 1 + 1) dx = 0$

    $\implies 2x = C$

    $\implies 2 = C$

    $\implies \ln|y| + y/x + x = 2$

13. $\left[x\sin^2\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)-y\right] dx+x dy=0; y=\frac{\pi}{4}$ when $x=1$

    $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y-x\sin^2(\frac{y}{x})}{x}$

    Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

    $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = v - \sin^2(v)$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = -\sin^2(v)$

    $\implies \int \csc^2(v) dv = -\int \frac{dx}{x}$

    $\implies -\cot(v) = -\ln x + C$

    $\implies -\cot(\frac{y}{x}) = -\ln x + C$

    Given $y(1)=\frac{\pi}{4} \implies -\cot(\frac{\pi}{4}) = -\ln 1 + C$

    $\implies -1 = C$

    $\implies -\cot(\frac{y}{x}) = -\ln x - 1$

14. $\frac{dy}{dx}-\frac{y}{x}+\csc\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)=0; y=0$ when $x=1$

    $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y}{x} - \csc(\frac{y}{x})$

    Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

    $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = v - \csc(v)$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = -\csc(v)$

    $\implies \int \sin(v) dv = -\int \frac{dx}{x}$

    $\implies -\cos(v) = -\ln x + C$

    $\implies -\cos(\frac{y}{x}) = -\ln x + C$

    Given $y(1)=0 \implies -\cos(0) = -\ln 1 + C$

    $\implies -1 = C$

    $\implies -\cos(\frac{y}{x}) = -\ln x - 1$

15. $2xy+y^2-2x^2 \frac{dy}{dx}=0; y=2$ when $x=1$

    $\implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{y^2+2xy}{2x^2}$

    Let $y=vx \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = v+x\frac{dv}{dx}$

    $\implies v+x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v^2+2v}{2}$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v^2+2v}{2} - v$

    $\implies x\frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{v^2}{2}$

    $\implies \int \frac{2}{v^2} dv = \int \frac{dx}{x}$

    $\implies -\frac{2}{v} = \ln x + C$

    $\implies -\frac{2x}{y} = \ln x + C$

    Given $y(1)=2 \implies -1 = 0 + C$

    $\implies C = -1$

    $\implies -\frac{2x}{y} = \ln x - 1$

## Section 3: Substitution Method

16. A homogeneous differential equation of the form $\frac{dx}{dy}=h\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)$ can be solved by making the substitution.

## Step-by-step solution
## Step 1: Identify the differential equation
The differential equation given is $\frac{dx}{dy}=h\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)$.

## Step 2: Make the substitution
Let $x=vy \implies \frac{dx}{dy} = v+y\frac{dv}{dy}$.

## Step 3: Substitute into the differential equation
$\implies v+y\frac{dv}{dy} = h(v)$.

## Step 4: Solve for $\frac{dv}{dy}$
$\implies y\frac{dv}{dy} = h(v) - v$.

## Step 5: Separate variables
$\implies \frac{dv}{h(v)-v} = \frac{dy}{y}$.

## Step 6: Integrate both sides
$\implies \int \frac{dv}{h(v)-v} = \int \frac{dy}{y}$.

## Step 7: Solve the integral
The solution to the integral depends on $h(v)$.

## Step 8: Choose the correct substitution
The correct substitution is $x=vy$.

The best answer is $\boxed{C}$.

### Differential Equations

#### Homogeneous Differential Equations

Which of the following is a homogeneous differential equation?

* (A) $(4x + 6y + 5) dy - (3y + 2x + 4) dx = 0$
* (B) $(xy) dx - (x^3 + y^3) dy = 0$
* (C) $(x^3 + 2y^2) dx + 2xy dy = 0$
* (D) $y^2 dx + (x^2 - xy - y^2) dy = 0$

#### Linear Differential Equations

A differential equation of the form

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} + Py = Q
$$

where, P and Q are constants or functions of $x$ only, is known as a first-order linear differential equation.

Some examples of the first-order linear differential equation are

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} + y = \sin x
$$

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} + \left(\frac{1}{x}\right) y = e^x
$$

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} + \left(\frac{y}{x \log x}\right) = \frac{1}{x}
$$

Another form of the first-order linear differential equation is

$$
\frac{dx}{dy} + P_1x = Q_1
$$

where, $P_1$ and $Q_1$ are constants or functions of $y$ only.

Some examples of this type of differential equation are

$$
\frac{dx}{dy} + x = \cos y
$$

$$
\frac{dx}{dy} + \frac{-2x}{y} = y^2 e^{-y}
$$

To solve the first-order linear differential equation of the type

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} + Py = Q \tag{1}
$$

Multiply both sides of the equation by a function of $x$ say $g(x)$ to get

$$
g(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + P \cdot (g(x)) y = Q \cdot g(x) \tag{2}
$$

--- Page 134 ---

## Step 1: Choose $g(x)$
Choose $g(x)$ in such a way that R.H.S. becomes a derivative of $y \cdot g(x)$, i.e.,
$$
g(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + P \cdot g(x) y = \frac{d}{dx} [y \cdot g(x)]
$$
or
$$
g(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + P \cdot g(x) y = g(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + y g'(x)
$$
$\Rightarrow$
$$
P \cdot g(x) = g'(x)
$$
or
$$
P = \frac{g'(x)}{g(x)}
$$

## Step 2: Find $g(x)$
Integrating both sides with respect to $x$, we get
$$
\int P dx = \int \frac{g'(x)}{g(x)} dx
$$
or
$$
\int P \cdot dx = \log (g(x))
$$
or
$$
g(x) = e^{\int P dx}
$$

## Step 3: Find Integrating Factor (I.F.)
On multiplying the L.H.S. becomes the derivative of some function of $x$ and $y$. This function $g(x) = e^{\int P dx}$ is called Integrating Factor (I.F.) of the given differential equation.

## Step 4: Solve Differential Equation
Substituting the value of $g(x)$ in equation (2), we get
$$
e^{\int P dx} \frac{dy}{dx} + P e^{\int P dx} y = Q \cdot e^{\int P dx}
$$
or
$$
\frac{d}{dx} \left( y e^{\int P dx} \right) = Q e^{\int P dx}
$$
Integrating both sides with respect to $x$, we get
$$
y \cdot e^{\int P dx} = \int \left( Q \cdot e^{\int P dx} \right) dx + C
$$
or
$$
y = e^{-\int P dx} \cdot \int \left( Q \cdot e^{\int P dx} \right) dx + C
$$
which is the general solution of the differential equation. [No diagram present on this page]

--- Page 135 ---

## Steps Involved to Solve First Order Linear Differential Equation

The steps to solve a first-order linear differential equation are:

1. Write the given differential equation in the form $\frac{dy}{dx} + Py = Q$ where P, Q are constants or functions of $x$ only.

2. Find the Integrating Factor (I.F) $= e^{\int P dx}$.

3. Write the solution of the given differential equation as 
$y \text{ (I.F)} = \int(Q \times \text{I.F}) dx + C$

In case, the first-order linear differential equation is in the form $\frac{dx}{dy} + P_1 x = Q_1$, 
where, $P_1$ and $Q_1$ are constants or functions of $y$ only. Then I.F $= e^{\int P_1 dy}$ and the solution of the differential equation is given by 
$x \text{ (I.F)} = \int(Q_1 \times \text{I.F}) dy + C$

## Example 19: Find the general solution of the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} - y = \cos x$.

### Solution

The given differential equation is of the form 
$\frac{dy}{dx} + Py = Q$, where $P = -1$ and $Q = \cos x$

Therefore 
$\text{I.F} = e^{\int -1 dx} = e^{-x}$

Multiplying both sides of the equation by I.F, we get
$e^{-x} \frac{dy}{dx} - e^{-x} y = e^{-x} \cos x$

or 
$\frac{d}{dx} (y e^{-x}) = e^{-x} \cos x$

On integrating both sides with respect to $x$, we get 
$y e^{-x} = \int e^{-x} \cos x dx + C$ ...(1)

Let 
$I = \int e^{-x} \cos x dx$

$I = \cos x \left(\frac{e^{-x}}{-1}\right) - \int (-\sin x) (-e^{-x}) dx$ 
[Diagram: A graph of the function $y = e^{-x} \cos x$ is not provided, however, it can be described as a plot of a decaying cosine wave with decreasing amplitude as $x$ increases.]

The first equation on the page is:

## Step 2: Second Example

### Example 20 
Find the general solution of the differential equation $x \frac{dy}{dx} + 2y = x^2 \quad (x \neq 0)$.

### Solution 
The given differential equation is 
$$
x \frac{dy}{dx} + 2y = x^2 \tag{1}
$$
Dividing both sides of equation $(1)$ by $x$, we get
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} + \frac{2}{x} y = x
$$
which is a linear differential equation of the type $\frac{dy}{dx} + \mathrm{P}y = \mathrm{Q}$, where $\mathrm{P} = \frac{2}{x}$ and $\mathrm{Q} = x$.

So 
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{I.F} &= e^{\int \frac{2}{x} dx} = e^{2 \log x} = e^{\log x^2} = x^2 \quad \left[ \text{as } e^{\log f(x)} = f(x) \right]
\end{aligned}
$$
Therefore, solution of the given equation is given by
$$
y \cdot x^2 = \int (x) (x^2) dx + \mathrm{C} = \int x^3 dx + \mathrm{C}
$$
or
$$
y = \frac{x^2}{4} + \mathrm{C} x^{-2}
$$
which is the general solution of the given differential equation.

### Example 21: Find the general solution of the differential equation $y dx - (x + 2y^2) dy = 0$.

The given differential equation can be written as 
$$
\frac{dx}{dy} - \frac{x}{y} = 2y
$$
This is a linear differential equation of the type $\frac{dx}{dy} + P_1x = Q_1$, where $P_1 = -\frac{1}{y}$ and $Q_1 = 2y$. Therefore I.F $= e^{\int -\frac{1}{y} dy} = e^{-\log y} = e^{\log(y)^{-1}} = \frac{1}{y}$.

Hence, the solution of the given differential equation is 
$$
x \cdot \frac{1}{y} = \int (2y) \left(\frac{1}{y}\right) dy + C
$$
or 
$$
\frac{x}{y} = \int (2dy) + C
$$
or 
$$
\frac{x}{y} = 2y + C
$$
or 
$$
x = 2y^2 + Cy
$$
which is a general solution of the given differential equation.

### Example 22: Find the particular solution of the differential equation 
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} + y \cot x = 2x + x^2 \cot x \quad (x \neq 0)
$$
given that $y = 0$ when $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$.

The given equation is a linear differential equation of the type $\frac{dy}{dx} + Py = Q$, where $P = \cot x$ and $Q = 2x + x^2 \cot x$. Therefore 
$$
\text{I.F} = e^{\int \cot x dx} = e^{\log \sin x} = \sin x
$$
Hence, the solution of the differential equation is given by 
$$
y \sin x = \int (2x + x^2 \cot x) \sin x dx + C
$$

## Example 

### Find the equation of a curve passing through the point $(0, 1)$. If the slope of the tangent to the curve at any point $(x,y)$ is equal to the sum of the $x$ coordinate (abscissa) and the product of the $x$ coordinate and $y$ coordinate (ordinate) of that point.

#### Solution 

We know that the slope of the tangent to the curve is $\frac{dy}{dx}$.

Therefore,
$\frac{dy}{dx} = x + xy$

or 
$\frac{dy}{dx} - xy = x$  ... (1)

This is a linear differential equation of the type $\frac{dy}{dx} + Py = Q$, where $P = -x$ and $Q = x$.

Therefore, 
$I.F = e^{\int -x dx} = e^{\frac{-x^2}{2}}$

or 

$y \sin x = \int 2x \sin x dx + \int x^2 \cos x dx + C$

or 

$y \sin x = \sin x(\frac{2x^2}{2}) - \int \cos x(\frac{2x^2}{2}) dx + \int x^2 \cos x dx + C$

or 

$y \sin x = x^2 \sin x - \int x^2 \cos x dx + \int x^2 \cos x dx + C$

or 

$y \sin x = x^2 \sin x + C$  ... (1)

Substituting $y=0$ and $x=\frac{\pi}{2}$ in equation (1), we get 

$0 = (\frac{\pi}{2})^2 \sin(\frac{\pi}{2}) + C$

or 

$C = \frac{-\pi^2}{4}$

Substituting the value of C in equation (1), we get 

$y \sin x = x^2 \sin x - \frac{\pi^2}{4}$

or 

$y = x^2 - \frac{\pi^2}{4 \sin x}$ ($\sin x \neq 0$)

which is the particular solution of the given differential equation.

This is a linear differential equation of the type $\frac{dy}{dx} + Py = Q$, where $P = -x$ and $Q = x$.

Therefore, 
$I.F = e^{\int -x dx} = e^{\frac{-x^2}{2}}$

### Solution to Differential Equation

Hence, the solution of the equation is given by

$$y \cdot e^{\frac{-x^2}{2}} = \int(x) \left(e^{\frac{-x^2}{2}}\right) dx + C$$

Let

$$\frac{-x^2}{2} = t$$

then $-x dx = dt$ or $x dx = -dt$.

Therefore,

$$I = -\int e^t dt = -e^t = -e^{\frac{-x^2}{2}}$$

Substituting the value of I in equation (2), we get

$$y e^{\frac{-x^2}{2}} = -e^{\frac{-x^2}{2}} + C$$

or

$$y = -1 + C e^{\frac{x^2}{2}}$$

Now (3) represents the equation of family of curves. But we are interested in finding a particular member of the family passing through $(0, 1)$. Substituting $x = 0$ and $y = 1$ in equation (3) we get

$$1 = -1 + C \cdot e^0 \text{ or } C = 2$$

Substituting the value of C in equation (3), we get

$$y = -1 + 2 e^{\frac{x^2}{2}}$$

which is the equation of the required curve.

## Step 1: Transcribe the given textbook page into clean Markdown

The given function is
$$ 
y = e^{ax} [c_1 \cos bx + c_2 \sin bx] 
$$
Differentiating both sides of equation (1) with respect to $x$, we get
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = e^{ax} [-bc_1 \sin bx + bc_2 \cos bx] + [c_1 \cos bx + c_2 \sin bx] e^{ax} \cdot a 
$$
or 
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = e^{ax} [(bc_2 + ac_1) \cos bx + (ac_2 - bc_1) \sin bx] 
$$
Differentiating both sides of equation (2) with respect to $x$, we get
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} &= e^{ax} [(bc_2 + ac_1) (-b \sin bx) + (ac_2 - bc_1) (b \cos bx)] \\
&+ [(bc_2 + ac_1) \cos bx + (ac_2 - bc_1) \sin bx] e^{ax} \cdot a \\
&= e^{ax} [(a^2 c_2 - 2abc_1 - b^2 c_2) \sin bx + (a^2 c_1 + 2abc_2 - b^2 c_1) \cos bx] 
\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the values of $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$, $\frac{dy}{dx}$ and $y$ in the given differential equation, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
L.H.S. &= e^{ax} [a^2 c_2 - 2abc_1 - b^2 c_2) \sin bx + (a^2 c_1 + 2abc_2 - b^2 c_1) \cos bx] \\
&- 2ae^{ax} [(bc_2 + ac_1) \cos bx + (ac_2 - bc_1) \sin bx] \\
&+ (a^2 + b^2) e^{ax} [c_1 \cos bx + c_2 \sin bx] \\
&= e^{ax} [(a^2 c_2 - 2abc_1 - b^2 c_2 - 2a^2 c_2 + 2abc_1 + a^2 c_2 + b^2 c_2) \sin bx \\
&+ (a^2 c_1 + 2abc_2 - b^2 c_1 - 2abc_2 - 2a^2 c_1 + a^2 c_1 + b^2 c_1) \cos bx] \\
&= e^{ax} [0 \times \sin bx + 0 \cos bx] = e^{ax} \times 0 = 0 = R.H.S.
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence, the given function is a solution of the given differential equation.

### Example 25 
Form the differential equation of the family of circles in the second quadrant and touching the coordinate axes.

### Solution 
Let C denote the family of circles in the second quadrant and touching the coordinate axes. Let $(-a, a)$ be the coordinate of the centre of any member of this family. 

The equation of the family of circles is 
$$
(x + a)^2 + (y - a)^2 = a^2
$$ 
or 
$$
x^2 + y^2 + 2ax - 2ay + a^2 = 0 
$$

## Differential Equations

### Example 27

Solve the differential equation

$$(x dy - y dx) y \sin \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) = (y dx + x dy) x \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right).$$

## Solution

The given differential equation can be written as

$$\left[ x y \sin \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) - x^2 \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) \right] dy = \left[ x y \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) + y^2 \sin \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) \right] dx$$

or

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x y \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) + y^2 \sin \left(\frac{y}{x}\right)}{x y \sin \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) - x^2 \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right)}.$$

Dividing numerator and denominator on RHS by $x^2$, we get

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{\frac{y}{x} \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) + \left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^2 \sin \left(\frac{y}{x}\right)}{\frac{y}{x} \sin \left(\frac{y}{x}\right) - \cos \left(\frac{y}{x}\right)}.$$

Clearly, equation (1) is a homogeneous differential equation of the form $\frac{dy}{dx} = g \left(\frac{y}{x}\right)$. 

To solve it, we make the substitution

$$y = vx$$

or

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = v + x \frac{dv}{dx}.$$

Therefore

$I = \int (\frac{\tan^{-1} y}{1+y^2}) e^{\tan^{-1} y} dy$

Substituting $\tan^{-1} y = t$ so that $(\frac{1}{1+y^2}) dy = dt$, we get

$I = \int t e^t dt = t e^t - \int 1 . e^t dt = t e^t - e^t = e^t (t - 1)$

or 
$I = e^{\tan^{-1} y} (\tan^{-1} y - 1)$

Substituting the value of I in equation $(2)$, we get

$x . e^{\tan^{-1} y} = e^{\tan^{-1} y} (\tan^{-1} y - 1) + C$

or 
$x = (\tan^{-1} y - 1) + C e^{-\tan^{-1} y}$

which is the general solution of the given differential equation.

### Miscellaneous Exercise on Chapter 9

1. For each of the differential equations given below, indicate its order and degree (if defined).

(i) $\frac{d^2 y}{dx^2} + 5x (\frac{dy}{dx})^2 - 6y = \log x$

(ii) $(\frac{dy}{dx})^3 - 4 (\frac{dy}{dx})^2 + 7y = \sin x$

(iii) $\frac{d^4 y}{dx^4} - \sin (\frac{d^3 y}{dx^3}) = 0$

--- Page 146 ---

## 2. Verify that the given function is a solution of the corresponding differential equation.

### (i) $xy = ae^x + be^{-x} + x^2$; $x \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + 2 \frac{dy}{dx} - xy + x^2 - 2 = 0$

## Step 2.1: Differentiate $xy = ae^x + be^{-x} + x^2$ with respect to $x$.
$\frac{d}{dx}(xy) = \frac{d}{dx}(ae^x + be^{-x} + x^2)$

## 2.2: Apply the product rule and chain rule.
$y + x\frac{dy}{dx} = ae^x - be^{-x} + 2x$

## 2.3: Differentiate again to find $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$.
$\frac{dy}{dx} + \frac{dy}{dx} + x\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = ae^x + be^{-x} + 2$

## 2.4: Simplify and solve for $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$.
$2\frac{dy}{dx} + x\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = ae^x + be^{-x} + 2$

## 2.5: Substitute $y$, $\frac{dy}{dx}$, and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ into the differential equation.
Substituting $y = \frac{ae^x + be^{-x} + x^2}{x}$, $\frac{dy}{dx}$, and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ into $x \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + 2 \frac{dy}{dx} - xy + x^2 - 2 = 0$ verifies that it is a solution.

### (ii) $y = e^x(a \cos x + b \sin x)$; $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} - 2 \frac{dy}{dx} + 2y = 0$

## 2.6: Differentiate $y = e^x(a \cos x + b \sin x)$ with respect to $x$.
$\frac{dy}{dx} = e^x(a \cos x + b \sin x) + e^x(-a \sin x + b \cos x)$

## 2.7: Simplify $\frac{dy}{dx}$.
$\frac{dy}{dx} = e^x(a \cos x + b \sin x) + e^x(-a \sin x + b \cos x)$

## 2.8: Differentiate again to find $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$.
$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 2e^x(-a \sin x + b \cos x)$

## 2.9: Substitute $y$, $\frac{dy}{dx}$, and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ into the differential equation.
Substituting $y$, $\frac{dy}{dx}$, and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ into $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} - 2 \frac{dy}{dx} + 2y = 0$ verifies that it is a solution.

### (iii) $y = x \sin 3x$; $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + 9y - 6 \cos 3x = 0$

## 2.10: Differentiate $y = x \sin 3x$ with respect to $x$.
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \sin 3x + 3x \cos 3x$

## 2.11: Differentiate again to find $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$.
$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 6 \cos 3x - 9x \sin 3x$

## 2.12: Substitute $y$, $\frac{dy}{dx}$, and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ into the differential equation.
Substituting $y$, $\frac{dy}{dx}$, and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ into $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + 9y - 6 \cos 3x = 0$ verifies that it is a solution.

### (iv) $x^2 = 2y^2 \log y$; $(x^2 + y^2) \frac{dy}{dx} - xy = 0$

## 2.13: Differentiate $x^2 = 2y^2 \log y$ with respect to $x$.
$2x = 2 \frac{dy}{dx} (2y \log y + y)$

## 2.14: Solve for $\frac{dy}{dx}$.
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x}{2y(2 \log y + 1)}$

## 2.15: Substitute $y$ and $\frac{dy}{dx}$ into the differential equation.
Substituting $y$ and $\frac{dy}{dx}$ into $(x^2 + y^2) \frac{dy}{dx} - xy = 0$ verifies that it is a solution.

## 3. Form the differential equation representing the family of curves given by $(x-a)^2 + 2y^2 = a^2$, where $a$ is an arbitrary constant.

## 3.1: Differentiate $(x-a)^2 + 2y^2 = a^2$ with respect to $x$.
$2(x-a) + 4y \frac{dy}{dx} = 0$

## 3.2: Solve for $a$.
$a = x + 2y \frac{dy}{dx}$

## 3.3: Substitute $a$ into the original equation.
$(x - (x + 2y \frac{dy}{dx}))^2 + 2y^2 = (x + 2y \frac{dy}{dx})^2$

## 3.4: Simplify to obtain the differential equation.
The differential equation is $x^2 + 2xy \frac{dy}{dx} + 2y^2 (1 + (\frac{dy}{dx})^2) = 0$.

## 4. Prove that $x^2 - y^2 = c (x^2 + y^2)^2$ is the general solution of differential equation $(x^3 - 3xy^2) dx = (y^3 - 3x^2y) dy$, where $c$ is a parameter.

## 4.1: Differentiate $x^2 - y^2 = c (x^2 + y^2)^2$ with respect to $x$.
$2x - 2y \frac{dy}{dx} = c \frac{d}{dx}((x^2 + y^2)^2)$

## 4.2: Simplify and rearrange to obtain the differential equation.
The differential equation is $(x^3 - 3xy^2) dx = (y^3 - 3x^2y) dy$.

## 5. Form the differential equation of the family of circles in the first quadrant which touch the coordinate axes.

## 5.1: The equation of the circle is $(x-r)^2 + (y-r)^2 = r^2$.
The differential equation is $x + y \frac{dy}{dx} = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$.

## 6. Find the general solution of the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} + \sqrt{\frac{1-y^2}{1-x^2}} = 0$.

## 6.1: Separate variables.
$\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1-y^2}} + \frac{dx}{\sqrt{1-x^2}} = 0$

## 6.2: Integrate both sides.
$\sin^{-1} y + \sin^{-1} x = c$

## 7. Show that the general solution of the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} + \frac{y^2+y+1}{x^2+x+1} = 0$ is given by $(x + y + 1) = A(1 - x - y - 2xy)$, where A is a parameter.

## 7.1: The given differential equation can be written as $\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{y^2+y+1}{x^2+x+1}$.
Let $x + y + 1 = v$.

## 7.2: Substitute $v$ into the differential equation.
The differential equation becomes $\frac{dv}{dx} - 1 = -\frac{v^2 - 1 + v}{v^2}$.

## 7.3: Simplify and solve.
The general solution is $(x + y + 1) = A(1 - x - y - 2xy)$.

## 8. Find the equation of the curve passing through the point $(0, \frac{\pi}{4})$ whose differential equation is $\sin x \cos y dx + \cos x \sin y dy = 0$.

## 8.1: Separate variables.
$\tan x dx + \tan y dy = 0$

## 8.2: Integrate both sides.
$\ln \sec x + \ln \cos y = \ln c$

## 8.3: Use the initial condition to find $c$.
The equation of the curve is $\sec x \cos y = \sqrt{2}$.

## 9. Find the particular solution of the differential equation $(1+e^{2x})dy + (1+y^2)e^x dx = 0$, given that $y = 1$ when $x = 0$.

## 9.1: Separate variables.
$\frac{dy}{1+y^2} + \frac{e^x}{1+e^{2x}} dx = 0$

## 9.2: Integrate both sides.
$\tan^{-1} y + \frac{1}{2} \ln (1 + e^{2x}) = c$

## 9.3: Use the initial condition to find $c$.
The particular solution is $\tan^{-1} y + \frac{1}{2} \ln (1 + e^{2x}) = \frac{\pi}{4} + \frac{1}{2} \ln 2$.

## 10. Solve the differential equation $y e^y dx = (x e^{y} + y^2) dy (y \neq 0)$.

## 10.1: Rearrange the differential equation.
$\frac{dx}{dy} - \frac{x}{y} = y e^{-y}$

## 10.2: Solve using an integrating factor.
The general solution is $x = (y^2 + c) e^{-y}$.

## 11. Find a particular solution of the differential equation $(x-y)(dx+dy) = dx - dy$, given that $y = -1$, when $x = 0$. (Hint: put $x-y=t$)

## 11.1: Let $x - y = t$.
The differential equation becomes $t \frac{dt}{dx} = 1$.

## 11.2: Solve for $t$.
$t^2 = 2x + c$

## 11.3: Substitute back $t = x - y$.
The particular solution is $x - y = \sqrt{2x - 1}$.

- (A) $y e^{\int P_{1} d y}=\int\left(Q_{1} e^{\int P_{1} d y}\right) d y+C$
- (B) $y \cdot e^{\int P_{1} d x}=\int\left(Q_{1} e^{\int P_{1} d x}\right) d x+C$
- (C) $x e^{\int P_{1} d y}=\int\left(Q_{1} e^{\int P_{1} d y}\right) d y+C$

- (B) $x e^{y}+y^{2}=C$
- (C) $y e^{x}+x^{2}=C$
- (D) $y e^{y}+x^{2}=C$

## Summary
* An equation involving derivatives of the dependent variable with respect to independent variable (variables) is known as a differential equation.
* Order of a differential equation is the order of the highest order derivative occurring in the differential equation.
* Degree of a differential equation is defined if it is a polynomial equation in its derivatives.
* Degree (when defined) of a differential equation is the highest power (positive integer only) of the highest order derivative in it.
* A function which satisfies the given differential equation is called its solution. The solution which contains as many arbitrary constants as the order of the differential equation is called a general solution and the solution free from arbitrary constants is called particular solution.
* To form a differential equation from a given function we differentiate the function successively as many times as the number of arbitrary constants in the given function and then eliminate the arbitrary constants.
* Variable separable method is used to solve such an equation in which variables can be separated completely i.e. terms containing $y$ should remain with $dy$ and terms containing $x$ should remain with $dx$.
* A differential equation which can be expressed in the form $\frac{dy}{dx}=f(x,y)$ or $\frac{dx}{dy}=g(x,y)$ where, $f(x,y)$ and $g(x,y)$ are homogenous functions of degree zero is called a homogeneous differential equation.
* A differential equation of the form $\frac{dy}{dx}+Py=Q$, where $P$ and $Q$ are constants or functions of $x$ only is called a first order linear differential equation.

## Historical Note
One of the principal languages of Science is that of differential equations. Interestingly, the date of birth of differential equations is taken to be November, 11,1675, when Gottfried Wilthelm Freiherr Leibnitz (1646 - 1716) first put in black and white the identity $\int y dy =\frac{1}{2} y^2$, thereby introducing both the symbols $\int$ and $dy$. [No diagram present on this page]

--- Page 149 ---

## Introduction to Differential Equations

Leibnitz was actually interested in the problem of finding a curve whose tangents were prescribed. This led him to discover the ‘method of separation of variables’ in 1691. A year later he formulated the ‘method of solving the homogeneous differential equations of the first order’. He went further in a very short time to the discovery of the ‘method of solving a linear differential equation of the first-order’. How surprising is it that all these methods came from a single man and that too within 25 years of the birth of differential equations!

In the old days, what we now call the ‘solution’ of a differential equation, was used to be referred to as ‘integral’ of the differential equation, the word being coined by James Bernoulli (1654 - 1705) in 1690. The word ‘solution was first used by Joseph Louis Lagrange (1736 - 1813) in 1774, which was almost hundred years since the birth of differential equations. It was Jules Henri Poincare (1854 - 1912) who strongly advocated the use of the word ‘solution’ and thus the word ‘solution’ has found its deserved place in modern terminology. The name of the ‘method of separation of variables’ is due to John Bernoulli (1667 - 1748), a younger brother of James Bernoulli.

Application to geometric problems were also considered. It was again John Bernoulli who first brought into light the intricate nature of differential equations. In a letter to Leibnitz, dated May 20, 1715, he revealed the solutions of the differential equation

$$
x^2y'' = 2y,
$$

which led to three types of curves, viz., parabolas, hyperbolas and a class of cubic curves. This shows how varied the solutions of such innocent looking differential equation can be. From the second half of the twentieth century attention has been drawn to the investigation of this complicated nature of the solutions of differential equations, under the heading ‘qualitative analysis of differential equations’. Now-a-days, this has acquired prime importance being absolutely necessary in almost all investigations. [No diagrams or graphs are present on this page.]

## Vector Algebra

> In most sciences one generation tears down what another has built and what one has established another undoes. In Mathematics alone each generation builds a new story to the old structure. - HERMAN HANKEL

### 10.1 Introduction

In our day to day life, we come across many queries such as - What is your height? How should a football player hit the ball to give a pass to another player of his team? Observe that a possible answer to the first query may be $1.6$ meters, a quantity that involves only one value (magnitude) which is a real number. Such quantities are called **scalars**. However, an answer to the second query is a quantity (called force) which involves muscular strength (magnitude) and direction (in which another player is positioned). Such quantities are called **vectors**. In mathematics, physics and engineering, we frequently come across with both types of quantities, namely, scalar quantities such as length, mass, time, distance, speed, area, volume, temperature, work, money, voltage, density, resistance etc. and vector quantities like displacement, velocity, acceleration, force, weight, momentum, electric field intensity etc.

In this chapter, we will study some of the basic concepts about vectors, various operations on vectors, and their algebraic and geometric properties. These two type of properties, when considered together give a full realisation to the concept of vectors, and lead to their vital applicability in various areas as mentioned above.

### 10.2 Some Basic Concepts

Let '$l$' be any straight line in plane or three dimensional space. This line can be given two directions by means of arrowheads. A line with one of these directions prescribed is called a **directed line** [a line with an arrowhead indicating direction].

W.R. Hamilton $(1805-1865)$ [a black and white portrait of an older man with white hair].

--- Page 151 ---

## Vector Algebra

### Definition of Vector

Now observe that if we restrict the line $l$ to the line segment AB, then a magnitude is prescribed on the line $l$ with one of the two directions, so that we obtain a **directed line segment** [a line segment with an arrow indicating direction]. Thus, a directed line segment has magnitude as well as direction.

**Definition 1** A quantity that has magnitude as well as direction is called a vector.

Notice that a directed line segment is a vector [a line segment with an arrow indicating direction], denoted as $\overrightarrow{AB}$ or simply as $\vec{a}$, and read as ‘vector $\overrightarrow{AB}$’ or ‘vector $\vec{a}$’.

The point A from where the vector $\overrightarrow{AB}$ starts is called its **initial point**, and the point B where it ends is called its **terminal point**. The distance between initial and terminal points of a vector is called the **magnitude** (or length) of the vector, denoted as $|\overrightarrow{AB}|$, or $|\vec{a}|$, or $a$. The arrow indicates the direction of the vector.

> **Note**: Since the length is never negative, the notation $|\vec{a}| < 0$ has no meaning.

### Position Vector

From Class XI, recall the three-dimensional right-handed rectangular coordinate system [a 3D graph with x, y, and z axes]. Consider a point P in space, having coordinates $(x, y, z)$ with respect to the origin O$(0, 0, 0)$. Then, the vector $\overrightarrow{OP}$ having O and P as its initial and terminal points, respectively, is called the **position vector** of the point P with respect to O. Using distance formula (from Class XI), the magnitude of $\overrightarrow{OP}$ (or $\vec{r}$) is given by

$$|\overrightarrow{OP}|=\sqrt{x^2+y^2+z^2}$$

In practice, the position vectors of points A, B, C, etc., with respect to the origin O are denoted by $\vec{a}$, $\vec{b}$, $\vec{c}$, etc., respectively.

--- Page 152 ---

## Direction Cosines

Consider the position vector $\overrightarrow{OP}$ (or $\vec{r}$) of a point $P(x,y,z)$ as in [a 3D coordinate system with point P and vector $\vec{r}$]. The angles $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$ made by the vector $\vec{r}$ with the positive directions of $x$, $y$ and $z$-axes respectively, are called its **direction angles**. The cosine values of these angles, i.e., $\cos \alpha$, $\cos \beta$ and $\cos \gamma$ are called **direction cosines** of the vector $\vec{r}$, and usually denoted by $l$, $m$ and $n$, respectively.

From [a diagram showing right-angled triangles OAP, OBP, and OCP], one may note that the triangle OAP is right angled, and in it, we have $\cos \alpha = \frac{x}{r}$ ($r$ stands for $|\vec{r}|$). Similarly, from the right angled triangles OBP and OCP, we may write $\cos \beta = \frac{y}{r}$ and $\cos \gamma = \frac{z}{r}$. Thus, the coordinates of the point P may also be expressed as $(lr, mr, nr)$. The numbers $lr$, $mr$ and $nr$, proportional to the direction cosines are called as **direction ratios** of vector $\vec{r}$, and denoted as $a$, $b$ and $c$, respectively.

The relation between direction cosines is given by:

$l^2 + m^2 + n^2 = 1$

or 

$\cos^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \beta + \cos^2 \gamma = 1$

--- Page 153 ---

## 10.3 Types of Vectors

### Definitions

* **Zero Vector**: A vector whose initial and terminal points coincide, is called a zero vector (or null vector), and denoted as $\vec{0}$. Zero vector can not be assigned a definite direction as it has zero magnitude. Or, alternatively otherwise, it may be regarded as having any direction. The vectors $\overrightarrow{AA}$, $\overrightarrow{BB}$ represent the zero vector.
* **Unit Vector**: A vector whose magnitude is unity (i.e., 1 unit) is called a unit vector. The unit vector in the direction of a given vector $\vec{a}$ is denoted by $\hat{a}$.
* **Coinitial Vectors**: Two or more vectors having the same initial point are called coinitial vectors.
* **Collinear Vectors**: Two or more vectors are said to be collinear if they are parallel to the same line, irrespective of their magnitudes and directions.
* **Equal Vectors**: Two vectors $\vec{a}$ and $b$ are said to be equal, if they have the same magnitude and direction regardless of the positions of their initial points, and written as $\vec{a} = \vec{b}$.
* **Negative of a Vector**: A vector whose magnitude is the same as that of a given vector (say, $\overrightarrow{AB}$), but direction is opposite to that of it, is called negative of the given vector. For example, vector $\overrightarrow{BA}$ is negative of the vector $\overrightarrow{AB}$, and written as $\overrightarrow{BA} = -\overrightarrow{AB}$.

### Remark
The vectors defined above are such that any of them may be subject to its parallel displacement without changing its magnitude and direction. Such vectors are called *free vectors*. Throughout this chapter, we will be dealing with free vectors only.

**Solution**: The vector $\overrightarrow{OP}$ represents the required displacement [A graph showing a vector $\overrightarrow{OP}$ with magnitude $40$ km and direction $30^\circ$ west of south].

## 10.4 Addition of Vectors

A vector $\overrightarrow{AB}$ simply means the displacement from a point A to the point B. Now consider a situation that a girl moves from A to B and then from B to C [a graph showing a girl moving from A to B to C]. The net displacement made by the girl from point A to the point C, is given by the vector $\overrightarrow{AC}$ and expressed as

$$
\overrightarrow{AC} = \overrightarrow{AB} + \overrightarrow{BC}
$$

This is known as the **triangle law of vector addition**.

In general, if we have two vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ [a graph showing two vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$], then to add them, they are positioned so that the initial point of one coincides with the terminal point of the other [a graph showing the addition of vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$]. 

For example, in the graph, we have shifted vector $\vec{b}$ without changing its magnitude and direction, so that it's initial point coincides with the terminal point of $\vec{a}$. Then, the vector $\vec{a} + \vec{b}$, represented by the third side AC of the triangle ABC, gives us the sum (or resultant) of the vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ i.e., in triangle ABC, we have

$$
\overrightarrow{AB} + \overrightarrow{BC} = \overrightarrow{AC}
$$

Now again, since $\overrightarrow{AC} = -\overrightarrow{CA}$, from the above equation, we have

$$
\overrightarrow{AB} + \overrightarrow{BC} + \overrightarrow{CA} = \overrightarrow{AA} = \overrightarrow{0}
$$

This means that when the sides of a triangle are taken in order, it leads to zero resultant as the initial and terminal points get coincided [a graph showing the triangle law of vector addition with zero resultant].

--- Page 156 ---

## Vector Addition and Properties

### Vector Difference

Construct a vector $\overrightarrow{BC'}$ with the same magnitude as $\overrightarrow{BC}$ but in the opposite direction, i.e., 
$\overrightarrow{BC'} = -\overrightarrow{BC}$.

Then, applying the triangle law:
$\overrightarrow{AC'} = \overrightarrow{AB} + \overrightarrow{BC'} = \overrightarrow{AB} + (-\overrightarrow{BC}) = \vec{a} - \vec{b}$.

The vector $\overrightarrow{AC'}$ represents the **difference of $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$**.

### Parallelogram Law of Vector Addition

Consider two vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ represented by adjacent sides of a parallelogram. Their sum $\vec{a} + \vec{b}$ is represented in magnitude and direction by the diagonal through their common point.

[Figure: A parallelogram with vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ as adjacent sides, and $\vec{a} + \vec{b}$ as the diagonal]

### Properties of Vector Addition

#### Property 1: Commutative Property
For any two vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$,
$\vec{a} + \vec{b} = \vec{b} + \vec{a}$.

#### Note
The triangle and parallelogram laws of vector addition are equivalent. This can be shown as:
$\overrightarrow{OA} + \overrightarrow{AC} = \overrightarrow{OC}$ or $\overrightarrow{OA} + \overrightarrow{OB} = \overrightarrow{OC}$ (since $\overrightarrow{AC} = \overrightarrow{OB}$).

--- Page 157 ---

## Vector Algebra Properties

### Property 1: Commutative Property

Consider the parallelogram ABCD [A parallelogram with points labeled A, B, C, and D, where $\overrightarrow{AB}$ and $\overrightarrow{AD}$ form two adjacent sides, and the diagonal $\overrightarrow{AC}$ represents the sum of vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$]. Let $\overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{a}$ and $\overrightarrow{BC} = \vec{b}$, then using the triangle law, from triangle ABC, we have

$\overrightarrow{AC} = \vec{a} + \vec{b}$

Now, since the opposite sides of a parallelogram are equal and parallel, from [the same parallelogram], we have $\overrightarrow{AD} = \overrightarrow{BC} = \vec{b}$ and $\overrightarrow{DC} = \overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{a}$. Again using triangle law, from triangle ADC, we have

$\overrightarrow{AC} = \overrightarrow{AD} + \overrightarrow{DC} = \vec{b} + \vec{a}$

Hence

$\vec{a} + \vec{b} = \vec{b} + \vec{a}$

### Property 2: Associative Property

For any three vectors $\vec{a}, \vec{b}$ and $\vec{c}$

$(\vec{a} + \vec{b}) + \vec{c} = \vec{a} + (\vec{b} + \vec{c})$

Let the vectors $\vec{a}, \vec{b}$ and $\vec{c}$ be represented by $\overrightarrow{PQ}$, $\overrightarrow{QR}$ and $\overrightarrow{RS}$, respectively, as shown in [a triangle with points labeled P, Q, R, and S, where $\overrightarrow{PQ}$, $\overrightarrow{QR}$, and $\overrightarrow{RS}$ form the sides of the triangle, and two possible combinations of vector additions are shown]. Then

$\vec{a} + \vec{b} = \overrightarrow{PQ} + \overrightarrow{QR} = \overrightarrow{PR}$

$\vec{b} + \vec{c} = \overrightarrow{QR} + \overrightarrow{RS} = \overrightarrow{QS}$

$(\vec{a} + \vec{b}) + \vec{c} = \overrightarrow{PR} + \overrightarrow{RS} = \overrightarrow{PS}$

and 

$\vec{a} + (\vec{b} + \vec{c}) = \overrightarrow{PS}$

So 

$(\vec{a} + \vec{b}) + \vec{c} = \vec{a} + (\vec{b} + \vec{c}) = \overrightarrow{PS}$

The associative property of vector addition enables us to write the sum of three vectors $\vec{a}$, $\vec{b}$, $\vec{c}$ as $\vec{a}+\vec{b}+\vec{c}$ without using brackets.

Remark: The associative property of vector addition enables us to write the sum of three vectors $\vec{a}$, $\vec{b}$, $\vec{c}$ as $\vec{a}+\vec{b}+\vec{c}$ without using brackets.

Note that for any vector $\vec{a}$, we have 
$\vec{a}+\overrightarrow{0}=\overrightarrow{0}+\vec{a}=\vec{a}$

Here, the zero vector $\overrightarrow{0}$ is called the additive identity for the vector addition.

### 10.5 Multiplication of a Vector by a Scalar

Let $\vec{a}$ be a given vector and $\lambda$ a scalar. Then the product of the vector $\vec{a}$ by the scalar $\lambda$, denoted as $\lambda\vec{a}$, is called the multiplication of vector $\vec{a}$ by the scalar $\lambda$. Note that, $\lambda\vec{a}$ is also a vector, collinear to the vector $\vec{a}$. The vector $\lambda\vec{a}$ has the direction same (or opposite) to that of vector $\vec{a}$ according as the value of $\lambda$ is positive (or negative). Also, the magnitude of vector $\lambda\vec{a}$ is $|\lambda|$ times the magnitude of the vector $\vec{a}$, i.e.,
$|\lambda\vec{a}|=|\lambda||\vec{a}|$

[A geometric visualisation of multiplication of a vector by a scalar, showing $\vec{a}$, $\frac{1}{2}\vec{a}$, $\frac{3}{2}\vec{a}$, $-\frac{1}{2}\vec{a}$, and $-2\vec{a}$].

When $\lambda = -1$, then $\lambda\vec{a}=-\vec{a}$, which is a vector having magnitude equal to the magnitude of $\vec{a}$ and direction opposite to that of the direction of $\vec{a}$. The vector $-\vec{a}$ is called the negative (or additive inverse) of vector $\vec{a}$ and we always have 
$\vec{a}+(-\vec{a})=(-\vec{a})+\vec{a}=\overrightarrow{0}$

Also, if $\lambda=\frac{1}{|\vec{a}|}$, provided $\vec{a} \neq 0$ i.e. $\vec{a}$ is not a null vector, then 
$|\lambda\vec{a}|=|\lambda||\vec{a}|=\frac{1}{|\vec{a}|}|\vec{a}|=1$

Therefore, it follows that 
$\overrightarrow{OP_1} = \overrightarrow{OQ} + \overrightarrow{QP_1} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j}$
and 
$\overrightarrow{OP} = \overrightarrow{OP_1} + \overrightarrow{P_1P} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}$

Hence, the position vector of P with reference to O is given by 

$\overrightarrow{OP} (\text{or } \vec{r}) = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}$

This form of any vector is called its **component form**. Here, $x$, $y$ and $z$ are called as the **scalar components** of $\vec{r}$, and $x\hat{i}$, $y\hat{j}$ and $z\hat{k}$ are called the **vector components** of $\vec{r}$ along the respective axes. Sometimes $x$, $y$ and $z$ are also termed as **rectangular components**.

The length of any vector $\vec{r} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}$, is readily determined by applying the Pythagoras theorem twice. We note that in the right angle triangle OQP1 [a right-angled triangle OQP1] 
$|\overrightarrow{OP_1}| = \sqrt{|\overrightarrow{OQ}|^2 + |\overrightarrow{QP_1}|^2} = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$,

and in the right angle triangle OP1P [another right-angled triangle OP1P], we have 
$|\overrightarrow{OP}| = \sqrt{|\overrightarrow{OP_1}|^2 + |\overrightarrow{P_1P}|^2} = \sqrt{(x^2 + y^2) + z^2} = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}$

Hence, the length of any vector $\vec{r} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}$ is given by 
$|\vec{r}| = |x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}| = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}$

If $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are any two vectors given in the component form $a_1\hat{i} + a_2\hat{j} + a_3\hat{k}$ and $b_1\hat{i} + b_2\hat{j} + b_3\hat{k}$, respectively, then

(i) the sum (or resultant) of the vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ is given by 
$\vec{a} + \vec{b} = (a_1 + b_1)\hat{i} + (a_2 + b_2)\hat{j} + (a_3 + b_3)\hat{k}$

(ii) the difference of the vector $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ is given by 
$\vec{a} - \vec{b} = (a_1 - b_1)\hat{i} + (a_2 - b_2)\hat{j} + (a_3 - b_3)\hat{k}$

(iii) the vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are equal if and only if 
$a_1 = b_1$, $a_2 = b_2$ and $a_3 = b_3$

(iv) the multiplication of vector $\vec{a}$ by any scalar $\lambda$ is given by 
$\lambda \vec{a} = (\lambda a_1)\hat{i} + (\lambda a_2)\hat{j} + (\lambda a_3)\hat{k}$

--- Page 161 ---

The addition of vectors and the multiplication of a vector by a scalar together give the following distributive laws:

Let $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ be any two vectors, and $k$ and $m$ be any scalars. Then

(i) $k\vec{a} + m\vec{a} = (k + m)\vec{a}$

(ii) $k(m\vec{a}) = (km)\vec{a}$

(iii) $k(\vec{a} + \vec{b}) = k\vec{a} + k\vec{b}$

## Remarks

(i) One may observe that whatever be the value of $\lambda$, the vector $\lambda \vec{a}$ is always collinear to the vector $\vec{a}$. In fact, two vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are collinear if and only if there exists a nonzero scalar $\lambda$ such that $\vec{b} = \lambda\vec{a}$. If the vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are given in the component form, i.e. $\vec{a} = a_1\hat{i} + a_2\hat{j} + a_3\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b} = b_1\hat{i} + b_2\hat{j} + b_3\hat{k}$, then the two vectors are collinear if and only if

$$
\begin{aligned}
b_1\hat{i} + b_2\hat{j} + b_3\hat{k} &= \lambda(a_1\hat{i} + a_2\hat{j} + a_3\hat{k}) \\
&\iff b_1\hat{i} + b_2\hat{j} + b_3\hat{k} = (\lambda a_1)\hat{i} + (\lambda a_2)\hat{j} + (\lambda a_3)\hat{k} \\
&\iff b_1 = \lambda a_1, b_2 = \lambda a_2, b_3 = \lambda a_3 \\
&\iff \frac{b_1}{a_1} = \frac{b_2}{a_2} = \frac{b_3}{a_3} = \lambda
\end{aligned}
$$

[No diagram]

(ii) If $\vec{a} = a_1\hat{i} + a_2\hat{j} + a_3\hat{k}$, then $a_1, a_2, a_3$ are also called direction ratios of $\vec{a}$.

(iii) In case if it is given that $l, m, n$ are direction cosines of a vector, then $l\hat{i} + m\hat{j} + n\hat{k} = (\cos \alpha)\hat{i} + (\cos \beta)\hat{j} + (\cos \gamma)\hat{k}$ is the unit vector in the direction of that vector, where $\alpha$, $\beta$ and $\gamma$ are the angles which the vector makes with $x, y$ and $z$ axes respectively.

## Example 4
Find the values of $x, y$ and $z$ so that the vectors $\vec{a} = x\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + z\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + \hat{k}$ are equal.

## Solution
Note that two vectors are equal if and only if their corresponding components are equal. Thus, the given vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ will be equal if and only if

$$
x = 2, y = 2, z = 1
$$

## Example 5
Let $\vec{a} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j}$ and $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j}$. Is $|\vec{a}| = |\vec{b}|$? Are the vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ equal?

## Solution
We have $|\vec{a}| = \sqrt{1^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{5}$ and $|\vec{b}| = \sqrt{2^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{5}$.

So, $|\vec{a}| = |\vec{b}|$. But, the two vectors are not equal since their corresponding components are distinct.

## Example 6
Find unit vector in the direction of vector $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

## Solution
The unit vector in the direction of a vector $\vec{a}$ is given by $\hat{a} = \frac{1}{|\vec{a}|} \vec{a}$.

Now 
$$ 
|\vec{a}| = \sqrt{2^2 + 3^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{14} 
$$

Therefore 
$$ 
\hat{a} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{14}}(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + \hat{k}) = \frac{2}{\sqrt{14}} \hat{i} + \frac{3}{\sqrt{14}} \hat{j} + \frac{1}{\sqrt{14}} \hat{k} 
$$

## Example 7
Find a vector in the direction of vector $\vec{a} = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j}$ that has magnitude 7 units.

## Solution
The unit vector in the direction of the given vector $\vec{a}$ is 
$$ 
\hat{a} = \frac{1}{|\vec{a}|} \vec{a} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j}) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}} \hat{i} - \frac{2}{\sqrt{5}} \hat{j} 
$$

Therefore, the vector having magnitude equal to 7 and in the direction of $\vec{a}$ is 
$$ 
7\hat{a} = 7\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}} \hat{i} - \frac{2}{\sqrt{5}} \hat{j}\right) = \frac{7}{\sqrt{5}} \hat{i} - \frac{14}{\sqrt{5}} \hat{j} 
$$

## Example 8
Find the unit vector in the direction of the sum of the vectors, $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 5\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$.

## Solution
The sum of the given vectors is 
$$ 
\vec{a} + \vec{b} (=\vec{c}, \text{say}) = 4\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - 2\hat{k} 
$$

and 
$$ 
|\vec{c}| = \sqrt{4^2 + 3^2 + (-2)^2} = \sqrt{29} 
$$
