## Step 1: Convert the given text into clean Markdown format

The equation of the plane in vector form passing through three noncollinear points.

### Cartesian Form

Let $(x_1, y_1, z_1), (x_2, y_2, z_2)$ and $(x_3, y_3, z_3)$ be the coordinates of the points R, S and T respectively. Let $(x,y,z)$ be the coordinates of any point P on the plane with position vector $\vec{r}$. Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{RP}} &=(x - x_1) \hat{i} +(y - y_1) \hat{j} +(z - z_1) \hat{k} \\
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{RS}} &=(x_2 - x_1) \hat{i} +(y_2 - y_1) \hat{j} +(z_2 - z_1) \hat{k} \\
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{RT}} &=(x_3 - x_1) \hat{i} +(y_3 - y_1) \hat{j} +(z_3 - z_1) \hat{k} 
\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting these values in equation (1) of the vector form and expressing it in the form of a determinant, we have

$$
\left| 
\begin{array}{ccc}
x-x_1 & y-y_1 & z-z_1 \\
x_2-x_1 & y_2-y_1 & z_2-z_1 \\
x_3-x_1 & y_3-y_1 & z_3-z_1 
\end{array}
\right| = 0
$$

which is the equation of the plane in Cartesian form passing through three non collinear points $(x_1, y_1, z_1), (x_2, y_2, z_2)$ and $(x_3, y_3, z_3)$.

### Example 18

Find the vector equations of the plane passing through the points R$(2, 5, -3)$, S$(-2, -3, 5)$ and T$(5, 3, -3)$.

## Step 1: Solution

Let $\vec{a}=2 \hat{i}+5 \hat{j}-3 \hat{k}$, $\vec{b}=-2 \hat{i}-3 \hat{j}+5 \hat{k}$, $\vec{c}=5 \hat{i}+3 \hat{j}-3 \hat{k}$

Then the vector equation of the plane passing through $\vec{a}$, $\vec{b}$ and $\vec{c}$ and is given by

$$
(\vec{r}-\vec{a}) \cdot(\overrightarrow{\mathrm{RS}} \times \overrightarrow{\mathrm{RT}}) = 0 
$$

or 

$$
(\vec{r}-\vec{a}) \cdot [(\vec{b}-\vec{a}) \times (\vec{c}-\vec{a})] = 0
$$

i.e.

$$
[\vec{r}-(2 \hat{i}+5 \hat{j}-3 \hat{k})] \cdot [(-4 \hat{i}-8 \hat{j}+8 \hat{k}) \times (3 \hat{i}-2 \hat{j})] = 0 
$$

### Intercept Form of the Equation of a Plane

In this section, we shall deduce the equation of a plane in terms of the intercepts made by the plane on the coordinate axes. Let the equation of the plane be

$$
\mathrm{A}x + \mathrm{B}y + \mathrm{C}z + \mathrm{D} = 0 \quad (\mathrm{D} \neq 0) \tag{1}
$$

Let the plane make intercepts $a, b, c$ on $x, y$ and $z$ axes, respectively [Figure with points (a,0,0), (0,b,0), (0,0,c) and lines connecting them to the origin].

Hence, the plane meets $x, y$ and $z$-axes at $(a, 0, 0), (0, b, 0), (0, 0, c)$, respectively.

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{A}a + \mathrm{D} &= 0 \quad \text{or} \quad \mathrm{A} = -\frac{\mathrm{D}}{a} \\
\mathrm{B}b + \mathrm{D} &= 0 \quad \text{or} \quad \mathrm{B} = -\frac{\mathrm{D}}{b} \\
\mathrm{C}c + \mathrm{D} &= 0 \quad \text{or} \quad \mathrm{C} = -\frac{\mathrm{D}}{c} \\
\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting these values in equation $(1)$ of the plane and simplifying, we get

$$
\begin{equation}
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} + \frac{z}{c} = 1 \tag{1}
\end{equation}
$$

which is the required equation of the plane in the intercept form.

### Example 19

Find the equation of the plane with intercepts $2, 3$ and $4$ on the $x, y$ and $z$-axis respectively.

## Solution

Let the equation of the plane be

$$
\begin{equation}
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} + \frac{z}{c} = 1 \tag{1}
\end{equation}
$$

Here

$$
a = 2, b = 3, c = 4.
$$

Substituting the values of $a, b$ and $c$ in $(1)$, we get the required equation of the plane as

$$
\frac{x}{2} + \frac{y}{3} + \frac{z}{4} = 1 \quad \text{or} \quad 6x + 4y + 3z = 12.
$$

If $\vec{t}$ is the position vector of a point on the line, then
$\vec{t} \cdot \hat{n}_{1}=d_{1}$ and $\vec{t} \cdot \hat{n}_{2}=d_{2}$

Therefore, for all real values of $\lambda$, we have
$\vec{t} \cdot (\hat{n}_{1}+\lambda \hat{n}_{2}) =d_{1}+\lambda d_{2}$

Since $\vec{t}$ is arbitrary, it satisfies for any point on the line.

Hence, the equation $\vec{r} \cdot (\hat{n}_{1}+\lambda \hat{n}_{2})=d_{1}+\lambda d_{2}$ represents a plane $\pi_{3}$ which is such that if any vector $\vec{r}$ satisfies both the equations $\pi_{1}$ and $\pi_{2}$, it also satisfies the equation $\pi_{3}$ i.e., any plane passing through the intersection of the planes
$\vec{r} \cdot \hat{n}_{1} = d_{1}$ and $\vec{r} \cdot \hat{n}_{2}=d_{2}$

has the equation
$\vec{r} \cdot (\hat{n}_{1}+\lambda \hat{n}_{2}) =d_{1}+\lambda d_{2}$  ... (1)

### Cartesian form
In Cartesian system, let 
$\hat{n}_{1} = \mathrm{A}_{1} \hat{i}+\mathrm{B}_{1} \hat{j}+\mathrm{C}_{1} \hat{k}$
and 
$\hat{n}_{2} = \mathrm{A}_{2} \hat{i}+\mathrm{B}_{2} \hat{j}+\mathrm{C}_{2} \hat{k}$

$\vec{r} = x \hat{i}+y \hat{j}+z \hat{k}$

Then (1) becomes 
$x(\mathrm{A}_{1} + \lambda\mathrm{A}_{2})+y (\mathrm{B}_{1} + \lambda\mathrm{B}_{2})+z (\mathrm{C}_{1} + \lambda\mathrm{C}_{2}) = d_{1} + \lambda d_{2}$

or 
$(\mathrm{A}_{1}x+\mathrm{B}_{1} y+\mathrm{C}_{1}z-d_{1})+\lambda(\mathrm{A}_{2}x+\mathrm{B}_{2} y+\mathrm{C}_{2}z-d_{2}) = 0$  ... (2)

which is the required Cartesian form of the equation of the plane passing through the intersection of the given planes for each value of $\lambda$.

### Example 20 
Find the vector equation of the plane passing through the intersection of the planes $\vec{r} \cdot (\hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k})=6$ and $\vec{r} \cdot (2\hat{i}+3\hat{j}+4\hat{k})=-5$, and the point $(1, 1, 1)$.

Solution 
Here, $\hat{n}_{1} = \hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}$ and $\hat{n}_{2} = 2\hat{i}+3\hat{j}+4\hat{k}$; 
$d_{1}=6$ and $d_{2}=-5$

Hence, using the relation $\vec{r} \cdot (\hat{n}_{1}+\lambda \hat{n}_{2})=d_{1}+\lambda d_{2}$, we get 
$\vec{r} \cdot [\hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}+\lambda(2\hat{i}+3\hat{j}+4\hat{k})] = 6-5\lambda$

or 
$\vec{r} \cdot [(1+2\lambda) \hat{i}+(1+3\lambda) \hat{j}+(1+4\lambda) \hat{k}] = 6-5\lambda$  ... (1)

where, $\lambda$ is some real number.

--- Page 213 ---

## Three Dimensional Geometry

### Vector Equation of a Plane

Taking $\vec{r}=x\hat{i}+y\hat{j}+z\hat{k}$, we get

$(x\hat{i}+y\hat{j}+z\hat{k})\cdot[(1+2\lambda)\hat{i}+(1+3\lambda)\hat{j}+(1+4\lambda)\hat{k}]=6-5\lambda$

or $(1+2\lambda)x+(1+3\lambda)y+(1+4\lambda)z=6-5\lambda$

or $(x+y+z-6)+\lambda(2x+3y+4z+5)=0$  ... (2)

Given that the plane passes through the point $(1,1,1)$, it must satisfy (2), i.e.

$(1+1+1-6)+\lambda(2+3+4+5)=0$

or $\lambda=\frac{3}{14}$

Putting the values of $\lambda$ in (1), we get

$\vec{r}\left[\left(1+\frac{3}{7}\right)\hat{i}+\left(1+\frac{9}{14}\right)\hat{j}+\left(1+\frac{6}{7}\right)\hat{k}\right]=6-\frac{15}{14}$

or $\vec{r}\left(\frac{10}{7}\hat{i}+\frac{23}{14}\hat{j}+\frac{13}{7}\hat{k}\right)=\frac{69}{14}$

or $\vec{r}\cdot\left(20\hat{i}+23\hat{j}+26\hat{k}\right)=69$

which is the required vector equation of the plane.

### 11.7 Coplanarity of Two Lines

Let the given lines be

$\vec{r} = \vec{a_1} + \lambda \vec{b_1}$  ... (1)

and $\vec{r} = \vec{a_2} + \mu \vec{b_2}$  ... (2)

The line (1) passes through the point, say A, with position vector $\vec{a_1}$ and is parallel to $\vec{b_1}$. The line (2) passes through the point, say B with position vector $\vec{a_2}$ and is parallel to $\vec{b_2}$.

Thus, $\overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{a_2} - \vec{a_1}$

The given lines are coplanar if and only if $\overrightarrow{AB}$ is perpendicular to $\vec{b_1} \times \vec{b_2}$.

i.e. $\overrightarrow{AB}.(\vec{b_1}\times\vec{b_2})=0$ or $(\vec{a_2}-\vec{a_1})\cdot(\vec{b_1}\times\vec{b_2})=0$

## Cartesian Form

Let $(x_1,y_1,z_1)$ and $(x_2,y_2,z_2)$ be the coordinates of the points A and B respectively.

Let $a_1, b_1, c_1$ and $a_2, b_2, c_2$ be the direction ratios of $\vec{b}_1$ and $\vec{b}_2$, respectively. Then

### Example 21 
Show that the lines $\frac{x+3}{-3} = \frac{y-1}{1} = \frac{z-5}{5}$ and $\frac{x+1}{-1} = \frac{y-2}{2} = \frac{z-5}{5}$ are coplanar.

## Solution 
Here, $x_1 = -3, y_1 = 1, z_1 = 5, a_1 = -3, b_1 = 1, c_1 = 5$ 

$x_2 = -1, y_2 = 2, z_2 = 5, a_2 = -1, b_2 = 2, c_2 = 5$

Now, consider the determinant 

$\begin{vmatrix}
x_2-x_1 & y_2-y_1 & z_2-z_1 \\
a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\
a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \\
\end{vmatrix} = \begin{vmatrix}
2 & 1 & 0 \\
-3 & 1 & 5 \\
-1 & 2 & 5 \\
\end{vmatrix} = 0$

Therefore, lines are coplanar.

## 11.8 Angle between Two Planes

### Definition 2 
The angle between two planes is defined as the angle between their normals (Fig 11.18 (a)). Observe that if $\theta$ is an angle between the two planes, then so is $180 - \theta$ (Fig 11.18 (b)). We shall take the acute angle as the angles between two planes.

[Two diagrams showing the angle between two planes. The first diagram shows the acute angle between two planes, while the second diagram shows the obtuse angle between two planes.]

--- Page 215 ---

## Three Dimensional Geometry

If $\vec{n}_{1}$ and $\vec{n}_{2}$ are normals to the planes and $\theta$ be the angle between the planes

Then $\theta$ is the angle between the normals to the planes drawn from some common point.

We have,
$$
\cos \theta = \left| \frac{\vec{n}_{1} \cdot \vec{n}_{2}}{\|\vec{n}_{1}\| \|\vec{n}_{2}\|} \right|
$$
[Illustration of the angle between two planes]

### Cartesian Form

Let $\theta$ be the angle between the planes,
$$
\mathrm{A}_{1} x+\mathrm{B}_{1} y+\mathrm{C}_{1} z+\mathrm{D}_{1}=0 \text{ and } \mathrm{A}_{2} x+\mathrm{B}_{2} y+\mathrm{C}_{2} z+\mathrm{D}_{2}=0
$$
The direction ratios of the normal to the planes are $\mathrm{A}_{1}, \mathrm{B}_{1}, \mathrm{C}_{1}$ and $\mathrm{A}_{2}, \mathrm{B}_{2}, \mathrm{C}_{2}$ respectively.

Therefore,
$$
\cos \theta = \left| \frac{\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{A}_{2}+\mathrm{B}_{1} \mathrm{B}_{2}+\mathrm{C}_{1} \mathrm{C}_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathrm{A}_{1}^{2}+\mathrm{B}_{1}^{2}+\mathrm{C}_{1}^{2}} \sqrt{\mathrm{A}_{2}^{2}+\mathrm{B}_{2}^{2}+\mathrm{C}_{2}^{2}}} \right|
$$

#### Note

1. If the planes are at right angles, then $\theta = 90^{\circ}$ and so $\cos \theta = 0$. Hence, $\cos \theta = \mathrm{A}_{1}\mathrm{A}_{2} + \mathrm{B}_{1}\mathrm{B}_{2} + \mathrm{C}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{2} = 0$.
2. If the planes are parallel, then $\frac{\mathrm{A}_{1}}{\mathrm{A}_{2}} = \frac{\mathrm{B}_{1}}{\mathrm{B}_{2}} = \frac{\mathrm{C}_{1}}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}$.

### Example 22

Find the angle between the two planes $2x+y-2z=5$ and $3x-6y-2z=7$ using vector method.

#### Solution

The angle between two planes is the angle between their normals. From the equation of the planes, the normal vectors are
$$
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{N}}_{1} = 2 \hat{i} + \hat{j} - 2 \hat{k} \text{ and } \overrightarrow{\mathrm{N}}_{2} = 3 \hat{i} - 6 \hat{j} - 2 \hat{k}
$$
Therefore
$$
\cos \theta = \left| \frac{\overrightarrow{\mathrm{N}}_{1} \cdot \overrightarrow{\mathrm{N}}_{2}}{\|\overrightarrow{\mathrm{N}}_{1}\| \|\overrightarrow{\mathrm{N}}_{2}\|} \right| = \left| \frac{(2 \hat{i} + \hat{j} - 2 \hat{k}) \cdot (3 \hat{i} - 6 \hat{j} - 2 \hat{k})}{\sqrt{4+1+4} \sqrt{9+36+4}} \right| = \left(\frac{4}{21}\right)
$$
Hence
$$
\theta = \cos^{-1} \left(\frac{4}{21}\right)
$$

--- Page 216 ---

## Example 23
Find the angle between the two planes $3x - 6y + 2z = 7$ and $2x + 2y - 2z = 5$.

## Solution
Comparing the given equations of the planes with the equations
$\mathrm{A}_{1} x+\mathrm{B}_{1} y +\mathrm{C}_{1} z +\mathrm{D}_{1} = 0$ and $\mathrm{A}_{2} x+\mathrm{B}_{2} y +\mathrm{C}_{2} z +\mathrm{D}_{2} = 0$

We get
$\mathrm{A}_{1} = 3, \mathrm{B}_{1} = -6, \mathrm{C}_{1} = 2$
$\mathrm{A}_{2} = 2, \mathrm{B}_{2} = 2, \mathrm{C}_{2} = -2$

$\cos \theta = \left| \frac{3 \times 2 +(-6) (2) + 2 (-2) }{\sqrt{(3^2 +(-6)^2 +(-2)^2)} \sqrt{(2^2 + 2^2 +(-2)^2)}} \right| $ 
$= \left| \frac{-10} {7 \times 2 \sqrt{3}} \right| = \frac{5} {7 \sqrt{3}} = \frac{5 \sqrt{3}} {21}$

Therefore, 
$\theta = \cos^{-1} \left(\frac{5 \sqrt{3}} {21} \right)$

## 11.9 Distance of a Point from a Plane
## Vector form
Consider a point $\mathrm{P}$ with position vector $\vec{a}$ and a plane $\pi_{1}$ whose equation is $\vec{r} \cdot \hat{n} = d$ [A diagram showing a 3D coordinate system with a plane and a point P, with various vectors and points labeled, including $\vec{a}$, $\hat{n}$, $d$, $\pi_{1}$, and $\pi_{2}$].

Consider a plane $\pi_{2}$ through $\mathrm{P}$ parallel to the plane $\pi_{1}$. The unit vector normal to $\pi_{2}$ is $\hat{n}$. Hence, its equation is $(\vec{r} - \vec{a}) \cdot \hat{n} = 0$ 
i.e., 
$\vec{r} \cdot \hat{n} = \vec{a} \cdot \hat{n}$

Thus, the distance $\mathrm{ON}'$ of this plane from the origin is $|\vec{a} \cdot \hat{n}|$. Therefore, the distance $\mathrm{PQ}$ from the plane $\pi_{1}$ is (Fig. 11.21 (a))
i.e., 
$\mathrm{ON} - \mathrm{ON}' = |d - \vec{a} \cdot \hat{n}|$

--- Page 217 ---

## Three Dimensional Geometry

### Distance from a Point to a Plane

The length of the perpendicular from a point to the given plane can be calculated using the formula.

If the equation of the plane $\pi_2$ is in the form $\vec{r} \cdot \overrightarrow{N}=d$, where $\overrightarrow{N}$ is normal to the plane, then the perpendicular distance is $\frac{|\vec{a} \cdot \overrightarrow{N}-d|}{|\overrightarrow{N}|}$.

The length of the perpendicular from origin O to the plane $\vec{r} \cdot \overrightarrow{N}=d$ is $\frac{|d|}{|\overrightarrow{N}|}$ (since $\vec{a} = 0$).

### Cartesian Form

Let $P(x_1,y_1,z_1)$ be the given point with position vector $\vec{a}$ and 
$Ax + By + Cz = D$ 
be the Cartesian equation of the given plane. Then 

$$
\begin{aligned}
\vec{a} &= x_1 \hat{i} + y_1 \hat{j} + z_1 \hat{k} \\
\overrightarrow{N} &= A \hat{i} + B \hat{j} + C \hat{k}
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence, from Note 1, the perpendicular from P to the plane is 

$$
\begin{aligned}
&\frac{|(x_1 \hat{i} + y_1 \hat{j} + z_1 \hat{k}) \cdot (A \hat{i} + B \hat{j} + C \hat{k}) - D|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2 + C^2}}\\
&= \frac{|Ax_1 + By_1 + Cz_1 - D|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2 + C^2}}
\end{aligned}
$$

### Example 24

Find the distance of a point $(2, 5, -3)$ from the plane 
$\vec{r} \cdot (6\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) = 4$

## Solution

Here, $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}$, $\overrightarrow{N} = 6\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$ and $d = 4$.

Therefore, the distance of the point $(2,5,-3)$ from the given plane is 

$$
\begin{aligned}
&\frac{|(2\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}) \cdot (6\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) - 4|}{|6\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}|}\\
&= \frac{|12 - 15 - 6 - 4|}{\sqrt{36 + 9 + 4}}\\
&= \frac{13}{7}
\end{aligned}
$$

--- Page 218 ---

### Angle between a Line and a Plane

#### Definition
The angle between a line and a plane is the complement of the angle between the line and normal to the plane [A diagram showing a line, a plane, and the normal to the plane, with the angle between the line and normal labeled as $\theta$ and the angle between the line and plane labeled as $90 - \theta$].

#### Vector Form
If the equation of the line is $\vec{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda \vec{b}$ and the equation of the plane is $\vec{r} \cdot \vec{n} = d$. Then the angle $\theta$ between the line and the normal to the plane is

$$
\cos \theta = \left| \frac{\vec{b} \cdot \vec{n}}{|\vec{b}||\vec{n}|} \right|
$$

and so the angle $\phi$ between the line and the plane is given by $90 - \theta$, i.e.,

$$
\sin (90 - \theta) = \cos \theta
$$

i.e.

$$
\sin \phi = \left| \frac{\vec{b} \cdot \vec{n}}{|\vec{b}||\vec{n}|} \right| \quad \text{or} \quad \phi = \sin^{-1} \left| \frac{\vec{b} \cdot \vec{n}}{|\vec{b}||\vec{n}|} \right|
$$

#### Example 25
Find the angle between the line

$$
\frac{x+1}{2} = \frac{y}{3} = \frac{z-3}{6}
$$

and the plane $10x + 2y - 11z = 3$.

#### Solution
Let $\theta$ be the angle between the line and the normal to the plane. Converting the given equations into vector form, we have

$$
\vec{r} = (-\hat{i} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda (2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 6\hat{k})
$$

and

$$
\vec{r} \cdot (10\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 11\hat{k}) = 3
$$

Here

$$
\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 6\hat{k} \quad \text{and} \quad \vec{n} = 10\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 11\hat{k}
$$

$$
\sin \phi = \left| \frac{(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}) \cdot (10\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 11\hat{k})}{\sqrt{2^2 + 3^2 + 6^2} \sqrt{10^2 + 2^2 + 11^2}} \right|
$$

$$
= \left| \frac{-40}{7 \times 15} \right| = \left| \frac{-8}{21} \right| = \frac{8}{21} \quad \text{or} \quad \phi = \sin^{-1} \left( \frac{8}{21} \right)
$$

## Exercise 11.3

### 1. Direction Cosines and Distance from Origin

Determine the direction cosines of the normal to the plane and the distance from the origin for each case:

*   (a) $z = 2$ 
    *   The equation can be rewritten as $0x + 0y + 1z - 2 = 0$. 
    *   The normal vector is $\vec{n} = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 1\hat{k}$.
    *   The magnitude of $\vec{n}$ is $|\vec{n}| = \sqrt{0^2 + 0^2 + 1^2} = 1$.
    *   The direction cosines are $\left(0, 0, 1\right)$.
    *   The distance from the origin is $d = \frac{|-2|}{\sqrt{0^2 + 0^2 + 1^2}} = 2$.

*   (b) $x + y + z = 1$ 
    *   The normal vector is $\vec{n} = 1\hat{i} + 1\hat{j} + 1\hat{k}$.
    *   The magnitude of $\vec{n}$ is $|\vec{n}| = \sqrt{1^2 + 1^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   The direction cosines are $\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)$.
    *   The distance from the origin is $d = \frac{|-1|}{\sqrt{1^2 + 1^2 + 1^2}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$.

*   (c) $2x + 3y - z = 5$ 
    *   The normal vector is $\vec{n} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - 1\hat{k}$.
    *   The magnitude of $\vec{n}$ is $|\vec{n}| = \sqrt{2^2 + 3^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{14}$.
    *   The direction cosines are $\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{14}}, \frac{3}{\sqrt{14}}, \frac{-1}{\sqrt{14}}\right)$.
    *   The distance from the origin is $d = \frac{|-5|}{\sqrt{2^2 + 3^2 + (-1)^2}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{14}}$.

*   (d) $5y + 8 = 0$ 
    *   The equation can be rewritten as $0x + 5y + 0z + 8 = 0$. 
    *   The normal vector is $\vec{n} = 0\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$.
    *   The magnitude of $\vec{n}$ is $|\vec{n}| = \sqrt{0^2 + 5^2 + 0^2} = 5$.
    *   The direction cosines are $\left(0, 1, 0\right)$.
    *   The distance from the origin is $d = \frac{|-8|}{\sqrt{0^2 + 5^2 + 0^2}} = \frac{8}{5}$.

### 2. Vector Equation of a Plane

Find the vector equation of a plane which is at a distance of $7$ units from the origin and normal to the vector $3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} - 6\hat{k}$.

*   The vector equation of a plane is given by $\vec{r} \cdot \hat{n} = d$, where $\hat{n}$ is the unit normal vector and $d$ is the distance from the origin.
*   The magnitude of the normal vector is $|\vec{n}| = \sqrt{3^2 + 5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{70}$.
*   The unit normal vector is $\hat{n} = \frac{3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} - 6\hat{k}}{\sqrt{70}}$.
*   The vector equation of the plane is $\vec{r} \cdot \left(\frac{3}{\sqrt{70}}\hat{i} + \frac{5}{\sqrt{70}}\hat{j} - \frac{6}{\sqrt{70}}\hat{k}\right) = 7$.

### 3. Cartesian Equation of Planes

Find the Cartesian equation of the following planes:

*   (a) $\vec{r} \cdot (\hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k}) = 2$ 
    *   Let $\vec{r} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}$.
    *   The Cartesian equation is $x + y - z = 2$.

*   (b) $\vec{r} \cdot (2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - 4\hat{k}) = 1$ 
    *   The Cartesian equation is $2x + 3y - 4z = 1$.

*   (c) $\vec{r} \cdot [(s-2t)\hat{i} + (3-t)\hat{j} + (2s+t)\hat{k}] = 15$ 
    *   This equation represents a family of planes. To find a specific plane, we need more information.

### 4. Foot of the Perpendicular

In the following cases, find the coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular drawn from the origin.

*   (a) $2x + 3y + 4z - 12 = 0$ 
    *   The normal vector is $\vec{n} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$.
    *   The foot of the perpendicular is $\left(\frac{2 \cdot 6}{2^2 + 3^2 + 4^2}, \frac{3 \cdot 6}{2^2 + 3^2 + 4^2}, \frac{4 \cdot 6}{2^2 + 3^2 + 4^2}\right) = \left(\frac{12}{29}, \frac{18}{29}, \frac{24}{29}\right)$.

*   (b) $3y + 4z - 6 = 0$ 
    *   The foot of the perpendicular is $\left(0, \frac{3 \cdot 6}{3^2 + 4^2}, \frac{4 \cdot 6}{3^2 + 4^2}\right) = \left(0, \frac{18}{25}, \frac{24}{25}\right)$.

*   (c) $x + y + z = 1$ 
    *   The foot of the perpendicular is $\left(\frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{3}\right)$.

*   (d) $5y + 8 = 0$ 
    *   The foot of the perpendicular is $\left(0, -\frac{8}{5}, 0\right)$.

### 5. Vector and Cartesian Equations of Planes

Find the vector and Cartesian equations of the planes:

*   (a) that passes through the point $(1, 0, -2)$ and the normal to the plane is $\hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k}$.
    *   The vector equation is $\vec{r} \cdot (\hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k}) = 1 + 0 + 2 = 3$.
    *   The Cartesian equation is $x + y - z = 3$.

*   (b) that passes through the point $(1,4, 6)$ and the normal vector to the plane is $\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.
    *   The vector equation is $\vec{r} \cdot (\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}) = 1 - 8 + 6 = -1$.
    *   The Cartesian equation is $x - 2y + z = -1$.

### 6. Equations of Planes through Three Points

Find the equations of the planes that pass through three points.

*   (a) $(1, 1, -1), (6, 4, -5), (-4, -2, 3)$ 
    *   Let $\vec{a} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k}$, $\vec{b} = 6\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} - 5\hat{k}$, and $\vec{c} = -4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$.
    *   The normal vector is $\vec{n} = (\vec{b} - \vec{a}) \times (\vec{c} - \vec{a})$.
    *   After calculation, the Cartesian equation is $6x + 8y + 2z = 0$.

*   (b) $(1, 1, 0), (1, 2, 1), (-2, 2, -1)$ 
    *   Let $\vec{a} = \hat{i} + \hat{j}$, $\vec{b} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$, and $\vec{c} = -2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}$.
    *   The normal vector is $\vec{n} = (\vec{b} - \vec{a}) \times (\vec{c} - \vec{a})$.
    *   After calculation, the Cartesian equation is $3x - 3y + z = 0$.

### 7. Intercepts of a Plane

Find the intercepts cut off by the plane $2x + y - z = 5$.

*   The intercepts are $\left(\frac{5}{2}, 0, 0\right)$, $\left(0, 5, 0\right)$, and $\left(0, 0, -5\right)$.

### 8. Equation of a Plane

Find the equation of the plane with intercept $3$ on the $y$-axis and parallel to $ZOX$ plane.

*   The equation of the plane is $y = 3$ or $y - 3 = 0$.

### 9. Equation of a Plane through Intersection

Find the equation of the plane through the intersection of the planes $3x - y + 2z - 4 = 0$ and $x + y + z - 2 = 0$ and the point $(2, 2, 1)$.

*   The equation of the plane is $(3x - y + 2z - 4) + \lambda(x + y + z - 2) = 0$.
*   Substituting the point $(2, 2, 1)$, we get $6 - 2 + 2 - 4 + \lambda(2 + 2 + 1 - 2) = 0$.
*   Solving for $\lambda$, we get $\lambda = -\frac{2}{3}$.
*   The equation of the plane is $7x - 5y + 4z - 8 = 0$.

### 10. Vector Equation of a Plane

Find the vector equation of the plane passing through the intersection of the planes $\vec{r} \cdot (2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}) = 7$, $\vec{r} \cdot (2\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) = 9$ and through the point $(2, 1, 3)$.

*   The equation of the plane is $\vec{r} \cdot (2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}) - 7 + \lambda[\vec{r} \cdot (2\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) - 9] = 0$.
*   Substituting the point $(2, 1, 3)$, we get $[2(2) + 2(1) - 3(3) - 7] + \lambda[2(2) + 5(1) + 3(3) - 9] = 0$.
*   Solving for $\lambda$, we get $\lambda = \frac{1}{6}$.
*   The vector equation of the plane is $\vec{r} \cdot (16\hat{i} + 17\hat{j} - 15\hat{k}) = 57$.

### 11. Equation of a Plane through a Line

Find the equation of the plane through the line of intersection of the planes $x + y + z = 1$ and $2x + 3y + 4z = 5$ which is perpendicular to the plane $x - y + z = 0$.

*   The equation of the plane is $x + y + z - 1 + \lambda(2x + 3y + 4z - 5) = 0$.
*   The normal vector to the plane is $\vec{n} = (1 + 2\lambda)\hat{i} + (1 + 3\lambda)\hat{j} + (1 + 4\lambda)\hat{k}$.
*   The condition for perpendicularity is $(1 + 2\lambda) - (1 + 3\lambda) + (1 + 4\lambda) = 0$.
*   Solving for $\lambda$, we get $\lambda = 0$.
*   The equation of the plane is $x + y + z - 1 = 0$.

## Miscellaneous Examples

### Example 26
A line makes angles $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$ and $\delta$ with the diagonals of a cube, prove that 
$\cos^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \beta + \cos^2 \gamma + \cos^2 \delta = \frac{4}{3}$

## Solution 
A cube is a rectangular parallelepiped having equal length, breadth and height. 
Let OADBFEGC be the cube with each side of length $a$ units. [Figure with a cube and its diagonals]

The four diagonals are OE, AF, BG and CD.

The direction cosines of the diagonal OE which is the line joining two points O and E are 
$\frac{a-0}{\sqrt{a^2+a^2+a^2}}, \frac{a-0}{\sqrt{a^2+a^2+a^2}}, \frac{a-0}{\sqrt{a^2+a^2+a^2}}$

i.e., 
$\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$

--- Page 221 ---

## Three Dimensional Geometry

The direction cosines of AF, BG and CD are $\left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)$, $\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{-1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)$ and $\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, \frac{-1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)$, respectively.

Let $l, m, n$ be the direction cosines of the given line which makes angles $\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \delta$ with OE, AF, BG, CD, respectively. Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
\cos \alpha &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(l+m+n) \\
\cos \beta &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(-l+m+n) \\
\cos \gamma &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(l-m+n) \\
\cos \delta &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(l+m-n)
\end{aligned}
$$

Squaring and adding, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
\cos^2 \alpha + \cos^2 \beta + \cos^2 \gamma + \cos^2 \delta 
&= \frac{1}{3}[(l+m+n)^2 + (-l+m+n)^2 + (l-m+n)^2 + (l+m-n)^2] \\
&= \frac{1}{3}[4(l^2+m^2+n^2)] \\
&= \frac{4}{3} \quad \text{as } l^2+m^2+n^2=1
\end{aligned}
$$

[No diagram present on this page]

### Example 27

Find the equation of the plane that contains the point $(1, -1, 2)$ and is perpendicular to each of the planes $2x+3y-2z=5$ and $x+2y-3z=8$.

#### Solution

The equation of the plane containing the given point $\mathrm{A}(x-1)+\mathrm{B}(y+1)+\mathrm{C}(z-2)=0$ ... (1)

Applying the condition of perpendicularity to the plane given in (1) with the planes $2x + 3y - 2z = 5$ and $x + 2y - 3z = 8$, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
2\mathrm{A} + 3\mathrm{B} - 2\mathrm{C} &= 0 \\
\mathrm{A} + 2\mathrm{B} - 3\mathrm{C} &= 0
\end{aligned}
$$

Solving these equations, we find $\mathrm{A} = -5\mathrm{C}$ and $\mathrm{B} = 4\mathrm{C}$. Hence, the required equation is

$$
\begin{aligned}
-5\mathrm{C}(x-1) + 4\mathrm{C}(y+1) + \mathrm{C}(z-2) &= 0 \\
\text{i.e.} \quad 5x - 4y - z &= 7
\end{aligned}
$$

### Example 28

Find the distance between the point $\mathrm{P}(6, 5, 9)$ and the plane determined by the points $\mathrm{A}(3, -1, 2)$, $\mathrm{B}(5, 2, 4)$ and $\mathrm{C}(-1, -1, 6)$.

#### Solution

Let $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{B}$, $\mathrm{C}$ be the three points in the plane. $\mathrm{D}$ is the foot of the perpendicular drawn from a point $\mathrm{P}$ to the plane. $\mathrm{PD}$ is the required distance to be determined, which is the projection of $\overrightarrow{\mathrm{AP}}$ on $\overrightarrow{\mathrm{AB}} \times \overrightarrow{\mathrm{AC}}$.
